With gas at $4.47 per gallon, Trump set to relax Biden-era emissions standards
2026-09-28

With gas at $4.47 per gallon, Trump set to relax Biden-era emissions standards

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
The Trump administration on Monday plans to release new fuel economy standards that would relax regulatory requirements for automakers to control pollution from gasoline-powered cars and light trucks. President Donald Trump said on his Truth Social media account Saturday that the less stringent mileage requirements would “take the waste out of building cars in America” and save families “thousands on a new, beautiful and safe car,” while boosting auto production in the U.S. Last December, the National Highway Traffic Safety Administration projected that the new standards would set the industry fleetwide average for light-duty vehicles at roughly 34.5 miles per gallon in the 2031 model year, down from a projected 50.4 miles per gallon in 2031 under rules put in place by the Biden administration. Fuel economy requirements set out how far new vehicles need to travel on a gallon of gasoline. The change would further Trump’s promise to revoke policies that encouraged or created incentives for the production of electric vehicles. Since taking office, Trump has relaxed auto tailpipe emissions rules, repealed fines for automakers that do not meet federal mileage standards and terminated consumer credits of up to $7,500 for EV purchases. Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy said Saturday on his social media account on X that the changes would be announced Monday. The White House, Department of Transportation and NHTSA couldn’t immediately be reached for comment on the details of the new standards. General Motors, Stellantis, and Ford Motor, maker of the top-selling pickup truck, the F-150, also couldn’t be immediately reached for comment. The administration and automakers have said the new rules will increase Americans’ access to the full range of gasoline vehicles they need and can afford. The average new car in America sold for $50,089 in August, crossing over the $50,000 line for the first time since last December, according to Kelley Blue Book data. At the same time, Americans are spending more on gas as Washington’s war with Iran disrupts the global flow of fuel. The national average price for a gallon of gas was $4.47 on Sunday, up from $4.09 a month ago, according to the AAA motor club. Trump has repeatedly pledged to end what he falsely calls an EV “mandate,” referring incorrectly to President Joe Biden’s target that half of all new vehicle sales be electric by 2030. No federal policy has mandated auto companies to sell EVs. According to data from the automotive research group Edmunds, EVs accounted for 6.5% of new vehicle sales in February, down from 7.4% for all of 2025. News of the revised standards immediately drew rebukes from environmentalists. Dan Becker, director of the Center for Biological Diversity’s Safe Climate Transport Campaign, said that the final rule “ignores the feasibility of clean technology and the millions of fuel-efficient cars already on the road.” “Trump is tanking sensible mile per gallon standards at the worst possible time for consumers, who are getting hit with sky-high prices at the pump,” Becker said in a statement. “Consumers will pay the price for these reckless rollbacks while Trump’s Big Oil and Big Auto buddies reap the short-term profits.” Katherine García, director of the Sierra Club’s Clean Transportation for All campaign, vowed the environmental group would fight the rule and said that the loosening of fuel standards would “make driving more expensive too.” “Less fuel-efficient cars mean more gas burned, spending more at the pump, and dirtier air in our communities,” Garcia wrote in a statement. When the 2024 standards went into effect, NHTSA estimated they would save 14 billion gallons of gasoline from being burned by 2050. The agency also said that while new fuel-efficient vehicles cost more up front, savings on gasoline over the lifetime of the car or truck would more than make up for that. Without these standards, in 2035, cars could produce 22,111 more tons of carbon dioxide per year than under the Biden-era rules. It also translates to an extra 90 tons a year of deadly soot particles and 4,870 additional tons a year of smog components such as nitrogen oxides and volatile organic compounds emitted into the air in coming years. Mileage standards, known as the corporate average fuel economy, or CAFE, have been implemented since the 1970s energy crisis, and over time, automakers have gradually improved their vehicles’ average efficiency. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. White House jokes that Trump could sell weapons to China and Taiwan at the same time
2026-09-28

White House jokes that Trump could sell weapons to China and Taiwan at the same time

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
President Donald Trump has justified the pending U.S. arms sale to Taiwan to Chinese President Xi Jinping by downplaying it as just one of many sales Washington makes around the world — even asking Xi if he’d be in the market for U.S. arms, Trump’s ambassador to China said on Sunday. David Perdue said Trump raised the prospect of the arms sales from the U.S. to its ascendant geopolitical rival, an unlikely offer that if consummated would upend generations of U.S. policy toward China and Taiwan and potentially expose U.S. technology to a potential foe. Perdue, speaking to Shannon Bream on “Fox News Sunday” days after Trump hosted Xi for a state visit in Washington, didn’t specify when the offer was made. Bream had cited concerns from Republican and Democratic lawmakers over delayed aid packages and weapons orders to Taiwan, the self-ruled democracy, to defend itself from China’s threat to invade the island. Bream asked if the U.S. is denying arms to Taiwan to appease the Chinese president, who visited Trump last week. Perdue pushed back on the suggestion, stating that Trump “is as strong on Taiwan as I’ve ever seen anybody” and has sold it plenty of weapons. Perdue also said Trump and Xi continue to talk about future U.S. weapons sales to Taiwan. “But President Trump continues to say, ‘Hey, we sell arms to other people around the world,’” Perdue said. “He actually asked President Xi, would he like to buy some, at one point.” The U.S. is bound by its own laws to provide Taiwan with the means to defend itself. While not recognizing Taiwan as a country, the U.S. is the island’s strongest informal backer and arms provider. But multiple U.S. leaders have left ambiguous whether they would commit military force to defend the U.S. ally should China invade. China has pressed Washington to halt the arms sales, most prominently during a previous summit in May between Trump and Xi in Beijing. After that meeting, the United States paused the sale of a record-breaking $14 billion package — and Trump described arms sales to Taiwan as a “very good negotiating chip” with China. In their private talks at the White House last week, Xi pressed Trump to oppose Taiwan independence, according to Chinese state media. In Beijing, China’s foreign ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun did not confirm Perdue’s remarks when asked about the matter on Monday. But he told reporters that China has always strengthened its national defense capabilities based on its needs, and is committed to maintaining world peace and stability. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Malaysia waits until its durians are fully ripe — and fully smelly. China’s $7.5 billion market looks on hungrily
2026-09-28

Malaysia waits until its durians are fully ripe — and fully smelly. China’s $7.5 billion market looks on hungrily

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Malaysia is pushing to take durian, whose powerful smell delights its lovers and repels its haters, beyond its roadside stalls and family orchards to convince fans of the spiky fruit that its varieties are worth seeking out. Devotees savor durians like wine. For Malaysian ones, the taste of their rich and creamy flesh varies widely across types. Some are prized for their sweet custard and fruity notes, others for a hint of bitterness, an alcohol tinge or even a peanut butter flavor. Malaysians’ efforts to elevate the industry can be found in the capital, Kuala Lumpur, at a center devoted exclusively to the fruit and the growing variety of products made from it. At the Kuala Lumpur Durian Experience Centre, near the landmark Petronas Twin Towers, visitors venture into a replica durian orchard to discover the fruit’s culture and history. In another room, a theater show draws laughter. Downstairs, visitors enjoy platters and egg tarts crafted from it. Launched this summer after an investment of nearly $2 million, the two-story center spans about 1,900 square meters (20,450 square feet) and is open daily. Its durian shop welcomes guests from morning to midnight. Its founder, Edison Ang, said the center targets both local customers and tourists, especially those from China, the world’s biggest durian importer. He said Chinese palates have long been influenced by Thai durian culture. “What we actually hope for now is not to get people who don’t eat durian to start eating durian,” he said. “Instead, we hope consumers who are already eating durian from other countries will switch to Malaysian durian.” In 2025, China imported 1.87 million tonnes of fresh durian worth $7.5 billion, with volume jumping over six times in the past decade, according to its customs data. Thailand’s earlier entry helped it secure over half that market by value in 2025. Malaysia, which began exporting fresh durian to China in 2024, was dwarfed. But for the first half of 2026, the value of China’s fresh durian imports from Malaysia quadrupled compared with the same period last year, pointing to rapid growth. Unlike their rivals, Malaysian growers typically do not harvest early, instead collecting only naturally ripened fruit caught in nets or ropes as they fall. That’s the industry’s biggest advantage and also its biggest weakness, Ang said, as sellers have to race against time because the fruit’s shelf life is at most two to three days. Any delays would affect its quality. When his stock was held up for several days during customs checks, his refund rate climbed, he said. Durian sales are also sensitive to economic conditions, said Leron Yee, founder of DKing, whose business covers both export and domestic markets. When the Chinese economy performs well, his business improves, but if the buying power of Chinese consumers falls, he needs to find alternative markets, he said. “It is a luxury, not a necessity,” he said. Businesses are finding opportunities in what the fruit can become. Yee’s business sells durian paste, durian-flavored chocolate and mochi — and even mooncakes ahead of the Mid-Autumn Festival. DurianBB founder Adrian Choy’s company offers durian ice cream, desserts and dried durian at a cafe, alongside coffee flavored with the Musang King variety and other items. His brand also offers guided farm tours. In July, Choy’s brand went a step further and launched a durian flower perfume that smells sweet and floral. Choy said branching into other products is important because it will add value to the food itself and differentiate his business, even when others compete on price. “Food is food,” he said. “You cannot sell it like 50% more because your branding is different.” Across China, durian specialty shops have mushroomed, with some specializing in Malaysian varieties. In Beijing, the Jiang Sir durian shop sells platters priced roughly between 300 and 1,000 yuan (about $44 to $150), depending on the weight of the pulp and the varieties chosen. Its employee, Hou Yuxin, welcomed the growing number of similar shops and brushed off pressure from competition, saying it would help boost the market for Malaysian durians. She noted many people now want to enjoy durians during their trips to Malaysia. Back at Ang’s center in Kuala Lumpur, Henry Zhang was one of them. Zhang, a visitor from Shanghai who started eating durian during his university days, saw a durian no bigger than his palm for the first time. He said Malaysian ones were creamier than those from Thailand and had different flavors. He was impressed by the center offering more than just the fruit. “I may still eat durians from other countries. But after eating them, I may miss Malaysian ones even more,” Zhang said. Associated Press photographer Vincent Thian and video journalists Olivia Zhang and Wu Jia in Beijing contributed to this report. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved. Silicon Republic
How might brain stimulation impact how we develop new skills?
2026-09-28

How might brain stimulation impact how we develop new skills?

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Ned Jenkinson of the University of Birmingham and Matthew Weightman of the University of Oxford discuss how advancements in brain research might affect how we learn and grow our skillsets. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) Whether learning a new piano piece or adapting your tennis serve, acquiring physical skills depends on your brain’s ability to strengthen and refine neural connections. Researchers are exploring whether this process can be accelerated with technology. Scientists are particularly interested in the potential of non-invasive brain stimulation, a group of techniques that can alter brain activity without surgery. Some deliver weak electrical currents to the brain through electrodes placed on the scalp. Others use magnetic fields or focused ultrasound waves. Although they work in different ways, they all aim to temporarily change the activity of neural circuits. If these techniques can successfully enhance neuroplasticity, the brain’s ability to reorganise and form new connections during learning, then they could be of use anywhere where performance depends on learning complex movements, from sport and music to surgery and beyond. Researchers are also seeing if these technologies could help with learning non-physical skills, such as picking up a foreign language. Elite sport, professional gaming and high-performance workplaces could all become targets for these enhancements if they prove effective. Brain stimulation could also have a big role to play in medicine, helping patients recover physical skills lost through injury or disease, such as stroke. Studies suggest there’s a lot of potential here. But translating this potential into useful tech that reliably boosts learning physical skills remains a big challenge. Research into enhancing motor learning with electric or magnetic stimulation has been gaining momentum since the turn of the millennium, with early studies garnering considerable excitement. In a typical experiment, participants might learn a sequence of finger movements similar to practising scales on a piano while receiving stimulation over brain regions involved in movement. Other studies have examined how stimulation could be used for balance training or teaching sports-related skills or surgical techniques. Some of these experiments produced eye-catching results, finding that participants learned certain movement tasks faster or retained skills for longer if they underwent brain stimulation. But other studies failed to find benefits. And in some cases, researchers struggled to replicate the success of earlier promising experiments when repeating them. One reason for these mixed findings is that there’s no such thing as a universal “learning network” in our brains. Different skills rely on different combinations of areas near the surface of the brain as well as those deep within it. Additionally, people can respond very differently to the same stimulation. Factors such as age, anatomy, genetics and even baseline skill level may influence whether stimulation is beneficial. Add to that the infinite number of ways to apply stimulation, the picture becomes murkier. Despite these challenges, the field continues to evolve in its quest to enhance motor learning. For instance, rather than broadly stimulating the brain, researchers are increasingly targeting specific neural circuits involved in learning. This is partly thanks to advances in neuroimaging and computational modelling, which has allowed scientists to predict how electrical currents travel through a person’s brain. Newer brain stimulation technologies, such as focused ultrasound, can also now reach deep structures involved in skill acquisition. The goal is to use these technologies not simply to increase brain activity, but to influence the right neural circuit at the right time during learning. This idea builds on a fundamental principle of neuroscience, often summarised as “neurons that fire together, wire together”. When brain cells are repeatedly activated at the same time, the connections between them become stronger. By carefully timing stimulation to coincide with the movements made during practice, researchers hope to reinforce the neural pathways involved in learning a new skill. In principle, this could make stimulation more reliable and more effective than current approaches, but researchers are still fine tuning exactly how this would work. Important questions remain. Who would have access? Should stimulation be regulated in competitive environments such as sport? And how much evidence should be required before consumer devices are marketed to healthy users? These questions are becoming increasingly relevant as brain stimulation moves beyond the laboratory and clinic. A number of at-home devices are now available for people to buy. Some have received regulatory approval, as they’re indicated for treating medical conditions such as depression. But there’s also a growing market for devices for cognitive and performance enhancement. For these uses, no regulatory approval is needed. The technology is advancing rapidly, but evidence to support it and regulations governing it are still trying to catch up. Proper frameworks for its adoption may simply be bypassed by the ready possibility of “DIY” brain stimulation. For now, brain stimulation is unlikely to transform anyone into an overnight virtuoso or elite athlete. But as researchers develop increasingly precise ways of targeting the neural circuits that underpin learning, the prospect of enhancing human performance is shifting from science fiction towards scientific possibility. The challenge today is not simply learning how to influence the brain, but deciding where, when and why we should. Ned Jenkinson is a senior lecturer in human movement sciences at the School of Sport and Exercise Sciences at the University of Birmingham. His research incorporates a range of techniques including non-invasive brain stimulation, electrophysiological recording, eye-tracking, neuroimaging and behavioural techniques. He uses these techniques to investigate how the brain controls movement and how it allows us to learn new motor skills. Matthew Weightman is a postdoctoral researcher at the Oxford Centre for Integrative Neuroimaging in the Plasticity Group at the University of Oxford, led by Professor Heidi Johansen-Berg. He is broadly interested in the field of sensorimotor neuroscience. His current work focuses on the role of sleep to recovery after stroke. More specifically, he is interested in how we can improve sleep after a stroke, whether improved sleep in stroke patients relates to better functional recovery, and if physiological processes that occur during sleep can be enhanced post-stroke to boost consolidation. The ultimate aim of his research is to help inform and improve future rehabilitation strategies. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. One-third of Irish employees finding work in fields unrelated to studies

One-third of Irish employees finding work in fields unrelated to studies

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Indeed’s research found that many professionals are not employed in the industries that their education prepared them for. New research from job hunting platform Indeed has found that many employees are working in areas that are unrelated to their field of study. One-third of the 1,000 professionals and jobseekers surveyed for the research agreed this was the case, with just over half noting that their education directly does relate to their education. According to the report, the relationship between education and future careers varies significantly by profession, employees in the education field (75pc) and finance (69pc), healthcare (65pc) and IT and telecommunications (65pc) were found to be the most likely to say that their prior education is linked to their current career. Comparably, many of those who participated, who are working in retail, catering and leisure (49pc), manufacturing and utilities (47pc), travel and transport (42pc) and legal (42pc) would state that their education is unrelated to the career they work in today. Commenting on the research, Derek Diviney, the vice-president of Indeed, said, “There can be a huge amount of pressure on students to feel that choosing a college course or first job means choosing their entire career. “But these findings show that the reality is often much more flexible. For many people, their education does provide a direct route into the career they want, but for others, the skills and knowledge they gain take them somewhere they may not originally have expected.” While Indeed’s research shows that a high percentage (59pc) of those who contributed believe that their college or university prepared them well for future career building, the report also highlighted areas in which education may be falling behind. When queried on the aspects of college education that failed to prepare students for their professional lives, 32pc found that interview preparation had not been adequately covered. Nearly one-third said that writing a CV or application materials could be better explained, one-in-four would appreciate efforts to build a long-term career plan, 26pc would like help identifying jobs that match their skills and interests (26pc) and nearly one-quarter want to better understand available career paths. Putting the closet in the cloud is a complex challenge, one that attracts people with an entrepreneurial spirit. Informing, entertaining and connecting the world Join us to start Caring. Connecting. Growing together Diviney said, “That ability to recognise and apply skills is becoming increasingly important, particularly in a competitive and rapidly changing job market. A qualification can open doors, but students and graduates should also think about the wider skills they are developing, and how those could be valuable across different roles and industries. “Understanding your strengths, being able to communicate them clearly and remaining open to different opportunities can help people navigate a changing world of work and discover career paths they may not otherwise have considered.” Indeed’s research comes at a time when the Irish jobs market is considered particularly competitive, as unemployment is low and employers are becoming more cautious in their approach to hiring. Additional data from Indeed shows that graduate job postings in Ireland are down 9pc since 2024, “creating a more competitive market for those taking their first steps into the workforce”. Young people, aged between 18 and 24 were found to be facing a particular challenge as nearly one-in-four said they struggle to identify which jobs they should apply for. 21pc said that they also struggle to stand out from other applicants. More than half admitted to struggling to explain how they are the best fit in an application or interview, while 81pc, within this age cohort, said they have a dream job they would like to achieve down the line. With education and future job readiness in mind, SiliconRepublic.com, as part of its STEM career coverage, explored the topic of post-graduate education. Particularly, the steps a student can take to determine whether they are ready to join the professional ecosystem, or whether a post-graduate programme might give them an extra edge. Silicon Republic
Nvidia unveils new system to stop AI agents from misbehaving
2026-09-28

Nvidia unveils new system to stop AI agents from misbehaving

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Jensen Huang, co-founder and CEO of Nvidia. Image: Fortune Global Forum/Flickr (CC BY-NC-ND 2.0) Jensen Huang wants the industry to self regulate on AI safety, says new laws would be unnecessary. Nvidia has unveiled an open software platform that promises to better restrict AI agents from taking problematic actions in order to achieve its goals. ‘Open Agent Safety Platform’ – the latest from the Open Secure AI Alliance – aims to strengthen AI security from agent testing to deployment, with software that monitors and sets boundaries for agents running on CPUs. One part of the stack, the OpenShell software, runs agents in a sandbox and turns operators’ instructions into a defined policy. The software is compatible with third-party compute platforms, including those from Arm and Intel. Meanwhile, Sentry provides in-silicon security enforcement, giving it the ability to quarantine and stop misbehaving AI agents. The launch is a direct response from Nvidia to a new kind of security threat where AI agents take concerning leaps in liberty to access data. Last week, Australia reported one such incident, where OpenAI’s agents hacked into a government site and even rewrote some internal files. Later that week, the company confirmed similar incidents relating to a number of US government department websites. In all these cases, the agents were looking for information when they decided to circumvent security controls that stopped them from any further access. “AI’s extraordinary potential for society will only be realised if we solve AI safety,” said Jensen Huang, Nvidia’s founder and CEO. Huang, in an interview earlier this month, told CNBC that AI companies need to release safer products rather than push for better regulations. Both OpenAI and Anthropic, in recent weeks, made comments calling for stronger AI safety, governance and slower development. “Since roughly this summer, AI has been advancing drastically faster, driven primarily by AI’s growing ability to build the next generation of AI,” Anthropic’s CEO Dario Amodei wrote in a blogpost this month. The company has tapped Accenture to independently test its frontier AI developments in a first-of-its-kind third-party security evaluation. Meanwhile Sam Altman said he is delaying plans for an OpenAI public listing over issues around AI safety. “We got a lot of stuff to do, like meeting this moment of what is going to be required for safety and alignment, and how the industry and governments can work together,” he told Fortune. Axios reported over the weekend that leading AI companies are investigating “tens of thousands of incidents” where their models acted out of order. But Huang disagrees. In comments to CNBC, the CEO said: “The fact that we need new laws, new antitrust laws, or new regulations, so that these companies could do their fundamental engineering and do it properly before they release products, that is just completely unnecessary.” “AI safety is a real thing,” Huang said. “Engineering products so that they are safe for the world to use – that’s a real thing. And companies should innovate as fast as possible, but they should never innovate so fast as to release unsafe products.” His views come as US president Donald Trump recently called concerns around AI safety a “hoax” and rejected the idea of placing guardrails around the technology. Huang is on the Trump administration’s advisory board on science and technology. “As we continue to discover the frontier of AI capabilities, we must accelerate discovery at the frontier of AI safety,” the CEO said in a statement announcing the new open source platform. “[The] Nvidia Open Agent Safety Platform brings together industry, researchers and public-sector organisations to share best practices, align on evaluation methods and foster international cooperation. Together, we can raise the bar for global AI safety.” The chipmaker formed the Open Secure AI Alliance earlier this year after OpenAI’s Hugging Face breach sent shockwaves across the industry. The new alliance, with several dozen members, including Adobe, CrowdStrike, Hugging Face and Dell, wants to develop and share tools designed for enhanced AI safety and cybersecurity. Nvidia purchased Hugging Face for nearly $13bn earlier this month. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. OpenAI agents tamper with US government sites after Australian breach
2026-09-28

OpenAI agents tamper with US government sites after Australian breach

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Image: © Kristina Blokhin/Stock.adobe.com OpenAI told NYT that its models often turn to government websites as ‘authoritative sources of public information’. OpenAI’s agents tampered with a number of US government sites, the company admitted, in what is a new development, as top AI models bypass boundaries and act unpredictably. According to The New York Times, the technology juggernaut confirmed incidents relating to the US Department of Commerce and the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC), and said that it is still probing a potential breach at the Department of Education. Sources told the publication that the agents were attempting to gather data from these sites. While none of these latest admissions were data breaches according to OpenAI, the company admitted that its agents acted in a concerning manner. The agents also shared public data from the SEC on an online forum. None of the departments, however, reported a data breach or impact to its functions. “Most of the activity we’ve reviewed so far involved routine research tasks, such as accessing public web content to answer questions,” an OpenAI spokesperson told the publication. “Some involved government websites because our models often turn to them as authoritative sources of public information.” This latest admission comes just after Australian prime minister Anthony Albanese told the press last week that OpenAI’s agents hacked one of Australia’s government websites in a similar incident, after the bots were sent to conduct research into public medicine spending. “Our models attempted to look up answers and available statistics”, a spokesperson explained last week. “In the course of that, [they] took actions we did not intend.” Or as Albanese put it, the models deliberately bypassed boundaries and “didn’t accept ‘no’ for an answer”. OpenAI said that its agents didn’t get their hands on patient records during the Medicare breach, although the Australian government said it is launching a forensic investigation and a taskforce to review the matter on its own. OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and Dario Amodei, the head at Anthropic, have both been called to attend an Australian Senate inquiry on AI in relation to the incident and other concerns around the technology. Last week, the two addressed the United Nations Security Council, where they touted the benefits of AI while warning of its potential abuses. The two have, in recent weeks, made comments pushing for stronger AI safety and slower development. Concerns around AI effectively going “rogue” have taken centre stage in recent months after OpenAI’s agents hacked into Hugging Face and a third-party infrastructure provider. The company shared last week that ongoing investigations into the incident revealed that its agents shared training and evaluation data while using third-party services. This, it said, was “not an appropriate use of this data” and that these cases “occurred before [they] implemented the safeguards”. Independent research has already shown that advanced AI agents can take risky and deceptive actions without specific prompting. Axios reported over the weekend that leading AI companies are investigating “tens of thousands of incidents” where their models acted out of order. From left: Hans Langeveld, Aoibheann Daly and Maria Mecenero Image: Felix Schulke/Kabuja Filmproduction Aoibheann Daly has been recognised by the European Commission for her project and will go on to participate at Regeneron ISEF in 2027. Irish student and Stripe Young Scientist and Technology Exhibition (YSTE) winner Aoibheann Daly has won a core prize at the European Union Contest for Young Scientists 2026 (EUCYS). The ceremony in Kiel, Germany, which was held from 22 to 27 September, saw more than 140 young scientists from 36 European and international guest teams showcase their research across a range of scientific disciplines. Daly received the Special Jury Award for Youth, which is awarded directly by the European Commission and according to EUCYS, is given in recognition of the delivery of the best scientific projects presented during the contest. Her project, ‘GlioScope: Multi-task Deep Learning and Causal AI for Glioma and Glioblastoma Profiling’, utilises AI intelligence to help doctors to diagnose and treat brain cancer. The project aims to improve patient care, deliver significant healthcare benefits and save valuable time. Daly will go on to participate as a finalist at Regeneron ISEF 2027 in Los Angeles in the US. Commenting on the win, Daly said: “Receiving a core prize at EUCYS and being selected as a finalist for Regeneron ISEF 2027 is an incredible honour. To represent Ireland on the European stage was already a privilege, and I’m hugely grateful to the Stripe Young Scientist & Technology Exhibition, my teachers, family and everyone who supported me along the way.” Alison Ahern, the head of Stripe Young Scientist and Education Partnerships, added: “We are incredibly proud to see Aoibheann receive a core prize at EUCYS 2026, and to be selected to participate as a finalist at Regeneron ISEF 2027 in Los Angeles, California. “From the moment she presented her project at the Stripe Young Scientist & Technology Exhibition, it was clear that her work combined scientific rigour, innovation and real-world impact. To see that recognised on the European stage is a proud moment for Irish science, and reflects the extraordinary standard of young scientific talent in Ireland which we at Stripe are honoured to play a small part in.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Brazil’s Nubank in talks to acquire UK’s Monzo – reports

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Fast-growing UK digital bank Monzo is reportedly weighing up options to be acquired by Brazil’s Nubank, or to launch a major new funding round. According to reporting over the weekend by Sky News and the Financial Times, the UK’s digital banking player Monzo has been approached by Brazil’s Nubank about an acquisition that would value it at between £8bn and £10bn. Sky News reports that Monzo has been approached about a combination with Nu Holdings, which itself has a market capitalisation of around $65.5bn. While discussions are still at an early stage, Monzo has engaged investment bankers from Morgan Stanley and Qatalyst as advisers. The UK digital bank, which launched in Ireland in April, is also weighing an alternative option, according to Sky News, which would see it launch a new fundraising round targeting a value of more than £8bn, in order to support further expansion in Europe. The Financial Times reports that a third option is also on the table with Monzo in talks with several private equity firms about selling a large stake of up to 15pc in the business to secure cash for planned expansion. In its latest annual report, Monzo claims more than 15m customers, with 10.4m active users – almost have of which use Monzo as their primary bank. The news comes just days after it was announced that Monzo’s EU CEO Michael Carney is stepping down from his role to launch a new AI accessibility start-up. In December 2025, Monzo became the first digital bank to secure a full European banking licence through the Central Bank of Ireland. It launched in Ireland this April with around 100,000 users on a waitlist to use its services. Earlier this year, the UK fintech announced plans to double its workforce in the country – based out of Dublin – to 70 by mid-2027. The company employs 5,275 according to its latest annual report. It also has offices in London, Cardiff and Barcelona. In late March, the UK bank announced plans to shutter its US operations in order to focus on growth opportunities closer to home. SpaceX’s Starship Just Reached Orbit, in What Should Be a Major Success for the Company. But Its Stock Price Did the Last Thing You’d Expect
2026-09-28

SpaceX’s Starship Just Reached Orbit, in What Should Be a Major Success for the Company. But Its Stock Price Did the Last Thing You’d Expect

Futurism | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
SpaceX’s 14th test flight of its enormous Starlink spacecraft took off from the company’s test facilities in South Texas Monday this morning, blasting off in a huge and now-familiar enormous cloud of dust and debris. The world’s most powerful rocket roared into the sky, gaining speed just as planned. After reaching just over 171 miles and a ground speed of over 16,000 mph, SpaceX’s Dan Huot made the official call. “Starship is orbital,” he said, to rapturous cheers at SpaceX HQ. “Starship’s in space. It’s in orbit. That feels so good to say.” The successful orbital insertion marks a major step in the right direction for SpaceX’s development of its massively ambitious launch platform after three years and 13 explosive, full-scale test launches — in addition to plenty of prototypes that were obliterated on the launchpad. If you were expecting a major rally of the company’s stock after what it had set up as a masterstroke of success, though, you’ll be surprised. Instead of jumping when markets opened just after the orbit insertion, the company’s stock has now fallen by about a percentage point. Any major spike, akin to a rocket launching into the sky, was notably absent. After all, plenty of questions remain over the viability of the company’s massive launch platform and SpaceX still has to overcome plenty of major hurdles, like being able to reliable stick the landing for both its Super Heavy booster and Starship spacecraft, as well as proving that it can safely transport astronauts ahead of NASA’s ambitious return journeys to the Moon — deadlines that are coming up fast. Then there’s the financial viability of its AI business, which is still burning through an enormous amount of cash. SpaceX’s valuation remains sky-high — just under $2 trillion — despite nagging concerns over its ability to ever make a profit. At press time, SpaceX’s stock is actually down about three percent over the last five days, and even more since its IPO in mid-June. It was a launch not without plenty of drama. Roughly 20 minutes into the flight, there was some confusion whether SpaceX would have the spacecraft attempt an orbital injection burn, with engineers furiously poring over the data. The loss of one vacuum Raptor engine clearly had them rattled and second-guessing whether to go on and try to have Starship circle the Earth six times. The stakes are incredibly high. NASA is hoping to test the spacecraft ahead of its first Moon landing attempt in 2028. Being able to successfully reach orbit is a major hurdle SpaceX has finally overcome, setting the stage for an attempt to load astronauts into the spacecraft while in Earth’s orbit as soon as next year. SpaceX also released 26 Starlink satellites, paving the way for the company to rapidly ramp up the build-out of its broadband satellite megaconstellation. More on SpaceX: SpaceX Preparing for Most Important Launch in Company History on Monday
Futurism
You Know Those Three Brothers Who Buy Struggling News Sites and Turn Them Into AI-Powered Content Farms? It Turns Out One of Them Has Extensive Connections to Ghislaine Maxwell
2026-09-28

You Know Those Three Brothers Who Buy Struggling News Sites and Turn Them Into AI-Powered Content Farms? It Turns Out One of Them Has Extensive Connections to Ghislaine Maxwell

Futurism | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Earlier this month, we published a months-long investigation into a little-known company called Brown Brothers Media. The venture is run by three brothers — Justin Brown, Lachlan Brown, and Brendan Brown — who buy struggling online publications and turn them into AI-powered slop farms that post thousands of articles to scoop up massive traffic from platforms like Google, pushing unwitting readers away from real journalism in the process. The growth of the Brown brothers’ empire, we found, has been powered by extraordinary acts of dishonesty and manipulation. As it fired virtually all its writing staff after the release of ChatGPT, it created scores of fake reporters, subject matter experts, and relatable everyday characters, then used their bylines to pump out lazy AI articles that were often plagiarized from original journalism, based on completely made-up “personal experiences,” or so careless and flawed that it’s hard to believe anyone at the company even reads them before they’re published. It appears that the operation has been a resounding success. According to data we reviewed, Brown Brothers Media’s network of sites is estimated to pull in somewhere around 64 million visits per month. That’s an astounding amount of traffic, putting the Brown brothers in a league with large media organizations like NPR or the Washington Post — and making them a stunted yet fascinating new type of media power player in the AI slop era. After we ran the story, we were surprised to learn a further fact about the Brown brothers that casts their activities with AI and the media in a peculiar new light: a startup Justin Brown founded called Ideapod, which was later folded into Brown Brothers Media and is still operating today, had numerous connections to the orbit of deceased financier Jeffrey Epstein — and particularly his longtime associate Ghislaine Maxwell, who is currently serving 20 years in federal prison for a sex trafficking conviction stemming from her work procuring underage girls for the infamous predator. In response to detailed questions about this story, Justin strongly pushed back against any association with Epstein. “I had no relationship, contact or dealings whatsoever with Jeffrey Epstein,” he wrote via email. “I never met or communicated with Epstein, had any business, financial, social or other dealings with him, received funding from him, or visited his island. Ideapod never received funding or investment from Epstein.” On the subject of Maxwell, he was more circumspect. “I am not prepared to speculate from memory or provide a question-by-question reconstruction of thirteen-year-old business and social history,” he wrote. “Any statement or implication that I or Ideapod had a relationship with Epstein, received funding from him, or knew about or participated in the crimes of Epstein or Ghislaine Maxwell would be false.” Back in September 2013, according to a cache of photos available on Getty Images, Justin Brown was a guest of honor at a “cocktail reception” thrown by Maxwell at her multimillion-dollar Upper East Side Manhattan townhouse, which Epstein purchased for her in the year 2000. In the photos, Justin can be seen posing and chatting with the socialite and future convicted child sex trafficker, dressed in a gray suit and holding a wine glass. (The event was billed as being hosted by “Dom Pérignon & Ghislaine Maxwell”; LVMH, the luxury goods conglomerate that owns the vintage Champagne brand, didn’t respond to a request for comment.) Justin showed up to the party years after Epstein’s original 2008 conviction for soliciting girls as young as 14. At the time, Epstein had been released from prison and put on the sex offender registry. It was easy to Google news articles about his plea deal and his close relationship with Maxwell that dated back to the early 1990s. Maxwell had thrown the soirée to celebrate the launch of Ideapod, a social media company started by Justin that billed itself as promoting “positive change by allowing everyone to share powerful ideas with the world’s most influential organizations and thought leaders.” Justin and his cofounder, Mark Bakacs — also at the 2013 party, as pictured below, and who Justin described in a 2015 article for Inc as his “best friend from high school” — positioned Ideapod as a more enlightened competitor to other social media platforms like Facebook and Twitter. Another guest at the 2013 party was Katherine Keating, the daughter of the former Prime Minister of Australia Paul Keating. Like Maxwell, Keating was connected to Epstein. After Epstein’s 2019 arrest, unearthed video from 2010 appeared to show Keating at Epstein’s Manhattan home with Andrew Mountbatten-Windsor, then known as Prince Andrew, one of Epstein’s most notorious associates whose royal title was later stripped as the scandal deepened. Then, documents published in 2025 by the US Department of Justice revealed Keating exchanging friendly emails with Epstein and his staff to coordinate several meetings, with Keating at one point offering to bring her younger sister to lunch with Epstein and Woody Allen after she was told that the director — who’s faced his own child sex abuse allegations — liked “pretty women.” Keating was also involved with Ideapod. She was listed as part of the company’s team on an archived page from the startup’s site back around the time of Maxwell’s fête, and a few months later she announced a “series of 52 five-minute interviews” for the Huffington Post, set to be published by Global Grind — a hip hop site by Def Jam cofounder Russell Simmons, who later resigned after a storm of sexual assault allegations — and Ideapod. Photos from the party show her conversing with Justin and Bakacs. “I want the philosopher with the musician with the rapper with the director,” Keating said of the Ideapod collaboration. “That’s the only way it’s going to stay relevant… Young people might not want to hear what George Soros has to say, but if you couple him with Lady Gaga.” Also mingling at the Ideapod launch was Amir Dossal, a former United Nations official who served on the board of Maxwell’s environmental charity, the TerraMar Project, which reportedly raised $283,429 — and owed Maxwell $549,093 in loan repayments — while giving out just $874 in grants. Amid reporting in 2019 that it was facing an investigation by the FBI over whether it was linked to Epstein’s sex trafficking operation, TerraMar abruptly shut down. Dossal was also associated with Ideapod, serving as an advisor for the venture. (Dossal didn’t respond to a request for comment.) “Ideapod is a great place to exchange ideas and learn about other people’s views in a relaxed environment — without the ‘noise,'” Dossal later wrote in a testimonial for the startup. “I find it very refreshing to see so many great ideas and initiatives from across the world — with many brilliant suggestions for bringing about positive social change.” Several more TerraMar board members were at the party. Photos show Ariadne Calvo-Platero, the daughter of the British Lord Beaumont of Whitley and reportedly Maxwell’s “best friend” as of 2011, whose name appears in Epstein’s notorious “little black book“; Steven Haft, a lawyer and executive connected to cryptocurrencies and media who produced movies including “Dead Poets Society”; and Stuart Beck, a former UN ambassador for the Pacific island nation of Palau who passed away in 2016. One shot from the gathering at Maxwell’s house shows Calvo-Platero, Dossal, Justin, and Bakacs all socializing together. (Neither Calvo-Platero nor Haft responded to requests for comment.) Content from Ideapod’s site around the time of its launch has long been deleted, and is only sporadically backed up by the Internet Archive. One page that appeared on its site in advance of Maxwell’s launch party promoted an initiative called “APYouthNet,” which purported to “bring together people who share a passion for the subject of youth employment.” Another post, credited to “UNESCO [United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization] Bangkok,” promoted an initiative with the Thai Ministry of Education to “support youth, especially non-formal education students to obtain life skills.” (UNESCO Bangkok didn’t respond to a request for comment.) In the wake of the September party, Justin repeatedly promoted Maxwell and her endeavors on social media. In December 2013, he shared Maxwell’s TED Talk from earlier that year— titled “Sea Hope, Sea Change, Sea Future” — on Twitter, tagging TerraMar. Then in February 2014, he tweeted that he’d “just taken the I Love the Ocean pledge at the TerraMar Project.” Days later, he shared an Ideapod page titled “Know Much More About Ghislaine Maxwell” — it’s since been deleted, and no archived version seems to exist — and again tagged TerraMar. He continued to post about TerraMar throughout the evening, at one point sharing a photo of Maxwell seated in a room at the UN. “Ghislaine Maxwell of [the TerraMar Project] being graciously introduced by [Amir Dossal],” Justin wrote. “Two wonderful activists. cc [Ideapod.]” A YouTube video published by TerraMar shows Dossal introducing Maxwell at the event, along with Haft and Beck, the other TerraMar guests from Maxwell’s Ideapod party. “I’m now going to invite the person who has been the inspiration behind the Sustainable Oceans Alliance, who really has something called TerraMar Project,” Dossal tells the room. “She controls the oceans around the world. If you were to look at the website you have the opportunity to buy up parts of the ocean through her… I invite my dear friend, Ghislaine Maxwell, to tell us how we can own the oceans.” Justin and Ideapod kept hanging out with Maxwell in the real world. In March 2014, Ideapod’s Instagram posted a photo from another party where the future convicted sex trafficker made an appearance, smiling in a photo with the company’s founders while wearing a tiger print coat. The shot was captured at what was then Ideapod’s New York office, another guest in attendance told Futurism. A second photo posted by the startup showed Bakacs and Justin smiling and laughing with Keating. The next month, in April, Ideapod posted a grinning selfie of Justin and Maxwell on its Instagram. Maxwell is pictured smiling softly, her eyes shielded behind huge sunglasses. “Ideapod get together at the UN,” reads the caption, again tagging TerraMar. (“Well, this has aged like milk,” a commenter added years later.) A few weeks after that, in May 2014, Maxwell appeared in an interview with Katherine Keating — the member of Ideapod’s team from the first party with links to Epstein and Andrew Mountbatten-Windsor — that was prominently branded as “in association with Ideapod.” The interview was seemingly part of the collaboration Keating had referenced between Ideapod and the Huffington Post, where she was a contributing editor at the time and which published a version of the chat on its site. (The video also credited Francesco Carrozzini, an Italian photographer who was later romantically linked to the singer Lana Del Rey, and married to Bee Shaffer, the daughter of Anna Wintour.) “Does my hair look good?” Maxwell says at the start of the clip, fussing with her signature pixie cut and laughing. “If the ocean dies, I’m not sure what that outcome will be,” Maxwell tells Keating in the interview. “But definitely not good.” “Continue the conversation at Ideapod,” reads a closing slide on the interview, along with a link to Ideapod’s site. A spokesperson for the Huffington Post, which was later rebranded as HuffPost and purchased by BuzzFeed, downplayed the video, saying that Keating was never on staff or compensated for her work. In a statement to Futurism, Keating said that the interview was sponsored by Ideapod and that Justin had introduced her to Maxwell. “My only connection to Ideapod was its limited sponsorship of several episodes of an online interview series published on HuffPost,” she wrote. “I had no role in Ideapod as a business, have no knowledge of their business dealings and have had no contact with its founders since the sponsorship ended. For its September 2013 platform launch, Ideapod’s founders introduced me to Maxwell and asked me to interview her about TerraMar’s stated ocean-conservation work. The interview was to be screened at the launch event, which Maxwell hosted.” “My dealings with Maxwell were limited to the interview and launch event,” she added. “I had no other dealings or ongoing relationship with her and, at that time, had no knowledge of her relationship with Epstein.” We also reached out to Maxwell’s attorney with questions, but didn’t hear back. Ideapod seemed to struggle to settle on a business model. Over the years, it vacillated between promoting a social network for people with big ideas, a search engine that never seems to have materialized, something called “Ideapod Academy,” and even a 16-week course by a Brazilian shaman named Rudá Iandê. It also held real-world “salons” where Ideapod users could meet and discuss ideas like virtual reality and the “role that inspiration can play in the human connection.” There were soon signs that money was tight. In 2014, Ideapod launched a crowdfunding campaign on Indiegogo seeking backers — to support what it described as “Medium meets Twitter meets TED” — and ultimately raised only $53,613. According to an Ideapod employee at the time, Justin emailed staff about approaching attendees at Maxwell’s party to let them “know we’re fundraising and that we’re looking for the right partner who believes in the vision.” “Ghislaine has offered to continue hosting Ideapod events at her home and we’re exploring the best way to grow the community of early users,” Justin wrote in the message. Also in 2014, Ideapod sought financing from another figure with longstanding relationships to Epstein and Maxwell: Richard Branson, the English founder of Virgin Group, who once emailed the convicted sex offender encouraging him to “bring your harem!” to a meeting. Branson is also a figure from the TerraMar universe; he was named a “founding citizen” of the project, according to The New York Times, and praised TerraMar in a since-deleted Facebook post. (A spokesperson for Branson told us that the mogul had no role in TerraMar.) As Justin later wrote for Forbes, he and his cofounder Bakacs flew out to Necker Island, which is owned by Branson in the British Virgin Islands — about 34 miles as the crow flies from Epstein’s infamous private island of Little St. James, with the neighboring billionaires sometimes visiting each other — and which is famous for hosting invite-only retreats for entrepreneurs. “We were low on cash and had difficulty even making it to the island,” Justin recalled in Forbes. “Conventional wisdom dictated that we conserve what we had left and continue with business as usual in New York. Instead, we furiously raised more funds, because we were convinced that Ideapod would receive a warm reception. And it did. We met four new investors and Branson wrote this blog post about us, making the trip a huge success.” The blog post Justin linked to has since been deleted from Virgin’s site, but an archived version shows that in it, Branson enthused about Ideapod in strikingly vague terms, writing that the startup was “all about sharing ideas and collaborating on bringing them to life, which can only increase the chances of society coming up with more game changing concepts.” (The post was deleted when Virgin’s site was “replatformed in 2021,” Branson’s spokesperson told us.) In a Facebook video posted from Branson’s island, Bakacs projects his voice over music playing loudly in the background to ask Branson if he has a message for the “Ideapod community.” “I love Ideapod, and I loved reading about all the ideas that you came up with,” Branson says, sounding a little flustered. “In fact, I’m writing an article for the New York Times based on that.” (Branson does not appear to have published an article for the NYT that mentions Ideapod.) Branson’s spokesperson told Futurism that the billionaire “met Ideapod’s founders at an event for entrepreneurs hosted by a third-party, where Richard was invited to speak.” After meeting Justin and Bakacs, the spokesperson added, Branson was “offered the opportunity to take a stake in the business but did not invest and did not provide any funding.” “Any contact Richard and Joan Branson had with Epstein took place on only a few occasions more than twelve years ago, and was limited to group or business settings, such as a charity tennis event,” Branson’s spokesperson said. “When Epstein offered a charity donation, the Bransons asked their team to carry out due diligence before accepting the donation, which uncovered serious allegations. As a result of what the due diligence uncovered, Virgin Unite did not take the donation and Richard and Joan decided not to meet or speak with Epstein again. Had they had the full picture and information, there would have been no contact whatsoever — Richard believes that Epstein’s actions were abhorrent and supports the right to justice for his many victims.” Justin wasn’t done with the Caribbean, though. As we reported in our previous story, he and his brothers are now connected to an AI company called Cognitive AI Systems Limited, which is registered in the British Virgin Islands. Do you know anything about the business activities of the Brown brothers? Email us: tips@futurism.com. We can keep you anonymous. In 2015, Justin and Bakacs left New York to continue working on Ideapod in Los Angeles. By the next year, Bakacs had exited the business, according to his LinkedIn. Ideapod morphed into a digital publisher. “18 months ago, Ideapod was in serious trouble,” Justin wrote in a January 2018 Facebook post. “We had a lot going for us. Richard Branson was a supporter, thousands of people were passionate users and we had high profile investors backing the company. But our temporary successes blinded us from the reality that our debt levels were increasing, we didn’t have any viable revenue lines and the core product hadn’t improved in months.” “We were rapidly headed for complete and utter failure,” he wrote, adding that “in the midst of massive turmoil, we successfully completed a ‘pivot’ of the business, became cash flow positive, created a distribution network resulting in Ideapod becoming one of the world’s most trafficked media networks and created a platform to launch multiple revenue lines around the core mission of Ideapod: provide ecosystems of thought where ideas are surfaced, discussed and critically analyzed through dialogue.” Ideapod’s path to success, he said, was due to guidance by the shaman Rudá Iandê — the same guru who sometimes sold online courses on its site. “Rudá isn’t a false guru,” he wrote. “His framework is incredibly powerful. Over the last few years I’ve seen Rudá help so many people wake up to themselves and turn their lives around.” “For me,” Justin added, “this journey is only just beginning.” Indeed, Ideapod has had a lasting impact on the media industry — because it was a predecessor entity to Brown Brothers Media, the brothers’ current venture which buys struggling news sites and turns them into AI-powered content mills that attract immense traffic. Ideapod drew in Justin’s brothers early on. In 2014, his sibling Brendan Brown’s company The Expert Editor pledged a $1,000 prize for the “best ‘idea'” on Ideapod — a contest that was judged by Keating and Dossal, the guests at Maxwell’s party who were involved with Ideapod. By 2017, Lachlan Brown was working at Ideapod alongside Justin, where he was eventually listed as the site’s editor-in-chief as the social media platform transitioned into a media company. As time went on, the site started publishing articles that seemed to set the template that Brown Brothers Media would later use to churn out AI-generated “personal essays” about loneliness and pop psychology that it bylined using fabricated authors identified as senior citizens, parents, subject matter experts, and other nonexistent people. All three of the Brown brothers eventually made their way to Southeast Asia. In addition to The Expert Editor, Brendan was running a second company called Global English Editing. In 2016, Lachlan had founded Hack Spirit, a publisher devoted to pop psychology and spirituality content. In 2020, they folded all their sites into one venture: Brown Brothers Media, where they now present Ideapod as a cornerstone component of the enterprise. “Justin co-founded the digital media platform Ideapod and spent a decade at the intersection of ideas and audience,” reads Brown Brothers Media’s current corporate homepage. “When we pooled that experience under one roof, the model was clear: acquire publications with long histories of expertise and authority, and invest in them.” Ideapod is still listed as part of Brown Brothers Media’s portfolio, and its site is still operational. It has a weird design that replaces your mouse cursor with an undulating purple orb. Its ambitions of a social network or search engine long abandoned, it publishes a wash of spammy AI-generated articles about psychology and finance like the other sites in the brothers’ network, which have often specialized in clickbaity genres pioneered in the digital media world of the 2010s, when Ideapod and other early Brown brothers companies were first forged. In May, after we started reaching out to people connected to Brown Brothers Media with questions about its practices, Ideapod was one of its many sites that abruptly started to delete fake authors and the content they’d bylined, scrubbing away the evidence of its dishonesty without informing readers. As recently as February of this year, Ideapod’s “About” page explained that “we started in 2013 as a platform for sharing ideas. Over the years, we’ve evolved.” “The internet got louder, attention became currency, and most media optimized for engagement over substance,” it added. “We went the other direction.” On the same page, Ideapod claimed that its editor-in-chief was a man named “Theo Arden.” His “background in cognitive psychology,” his bio alleged, “shapes the publication’s approach to psychology and human behavior.” But Arden was yet another completely fabricated Brown Brothers Media persona — a character so lazily made that in his AI-generated portrait on the site, his thumbs intersected each other in an impossible way. In many of the articles he once bylined, in fact, he appeared as a completely different AI-generated person. “Theo guides Ideapod’s editorial direction with a focus on clarity, independent thinking,” Arden’s since-removed bio continued, “and respect for the reader’s intelligence.” Ideapod’s early proximity to Maxwell and the “Epstein class” recontextualizes Brown Brothers Media. Initially, we perceived Justin and his siblings to be opportunistic hustlers on the extreme fringe of the publishing industry, buoyed to startling heights of success when they discovered that buying sites trusted by Google would allow them to crank out AI sludge to an immense audience. But Justin’s links to Maxwell and Epstein’s circles put him — at least at one point — in far more elite echelons of finance and media. Then there’s the question of Ideapod’s finances. According to Pitchbook data, the startup raised about $3 million in funding over its years in business. Given its extensive connections to associates of Maxwell and Epstein, it’s hard not to wonder if any of Ideapod’s capital came from that network — or even the pedophile financier himself. For his part, Justin firmly denied any connection to Epstein. “Your questions appear to use associations with third parties, and even the geographic proximity of two islands, to imply a relationship with Epstein,” he wrote. “None existed.” “Ideapod never received money or investment from Maxwell,” he added in response to questions about the socialite. “I am not going to speculate about other people’s connections to her.” He declined to comment on the last time he had contact with Maxwell. Bakacs, Justin’s cofounder at Ideapod who now works as a cryptocurrency and blockchain “strategic advisor” in Brazil, also denied any connection to Epstein, and said that his interactions with Maxwell were extremely limited. Bakacs said that it was Justin who met Maxwell “at the UN, and she subsequently offered to host a single cocktail reception for Ideapod at her Manhattan townhouse. I met with her once beforehand to plan the event, and attended the reception itself. Neither Jeffrey Epstein nor Maxwell were on our radar at the time, and we were completely unaware of any past convictions or connections.” “To my recollection, my only in-person interactions with her were that single planning meeting and the reception itself,” he added. “Given that we were both active around the UN and related events at the time, it’s possible we were in attendance at some of the same UN related gatherings, but I had no other meetings or relationship with her.” When we pointed out the photo on Ideapod’s Instagram showing him hanging out with Maxwell at a later gathering, Bakacs didn’t respond. It’s tough to say what — if anything — Ideapod’s entanglements with Maxwell and other people connected to Epstein say about Brown Brothers Media and its current work publishing vast amounts of AI slop in pursuit of Google traffic. At the very least, Justin either didn’t perform basic research on Maxwell and her associates to discover their disturbing connections to a notorious sex criminal — or he did, and he just didn’t care. In either case, the carelessness feels telling. As Brown Brothers Media built its AI empire over the past few years, it peppered the sites it acquired with high-minded language about how it “chronicles the systems that shape our lives” and brings “clarity, insight, and depth to the topics that shape how we think, communicate, and live.” The reality, of course, is that it was deploying this pretentious language at the exact same time that it was filling these sites with nonexistent journalists and using them as puppets to byline garbage-tier content. It’s hard not to see a parallel to Justin heaping praise on Maxwell and milking her connections for publicity or funding, promising that Ideapod would “improve our world in unfathomable ways” while pointedly failing to notice that the entire milieu had a rotten stench. The closer you look, the more the Brown brothers start to feel like those “see no evil, hear no evil,” monkeys in tchotchke shops: talking a big game while staying willfully ignorant of exactly what’s made them relevant — whether it’s schmoozing with reprehensible people or pumping the web full of fake characters lamenting their loss of identity in old age or grieving the death of an imaginary loved one. Whatever else you can say about him, Justin is resilient. When Ideapod’s various business plans didn’t work out, he pivoted the company, eventually folding it into Brown Brothers Media. And by embracing AI at incredible scale, he seems to have finally earned the vast audience he’d long dreamed of, even if getting there meant lying to the readers whom Ideapod promised to enlighten and respect. “Changing the world used to be exceptionally hard,” he wrote in the Forbes article about visiting Richard Branson’s island. “One thousand years ago, only the kings or queens could make change at a national or regional level. One hundred years ago, it was the industrialists transforming society. Now, it’s anyone with an idea and Internet connection.” More on Brown Brothers Media: How Three Brothers Built an AI Slop Empire by Buying Legitimate News Sites and Turning Them Into Zombie Content Farms That They Say Get 50 Million Page Views per Month

All Major Social Networks Refusing to Promote Documentary That Made Elon Musk Mad
2026-09-27

All Major Social Networks Refusing to Promote Documentary That Made Elon Musk Mad

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
For the past few weeks, Elon Musk has been throwing a tantrum — and throwing around legal threats — over Alex Gibney’s “hit piece” documentary about him that’s set to release next month. Now, as if following his lead, pretty much all of the major social media sites are refusing to run ads for the movie, according to The Hollywood Reporter. The platforms — YouTube, TikTok, Instagram, and Facebook — are unwilling to accept paid advertising for the film. And of course so is X, the site owned by Musk himself. The stonewalling has reportedly taken distributor Bleecker Street by surprise, with the company scrambling to figure out why its ads have been so widely rejected. YouTube, TikTok, and Facebook and Instagram owner Meta have all provided a reason related to “political content” to the distributor for why they turned down the ad, per the reporting. This line of reasoning feels specious: although TikTok and Facebook by policy don’t allow political ads, neither the movie nor its trailer are part of a political campaign. It’s an even stranger argument for YouTube, which has no prohibitions on political advertising. If, as what appears to be happening, the tech industry is closing ranks around one of its own, it would be an astonishing testament to Musk’s influence and would represent an ominous threat to free speech. The film requires advertising to reach an audience, and if major social media platforms can collude to ice out works that criticize figures in their industry, it makes it so that there’s not only less of an audience seeing these critiques, but that producing these critical works is financially unsustainable. The nearly four-hour film, titled “Musk,” is a comprehensive and critical look at the billionaire’s life, beginning with his upbringing in South Africa and running through his success as a tech entrepreneur and his rise as a major and polarizing political figure. Musk has been raging against the film since it was first announced in 2023, calling it a “hit piece,” even though there were barely any details about it at the time. In the past month, he’s ramped up his attacks against the documentary, calling Gibney a “douchebag” and accusing him of being “incredibly biased.” He also, via his lawyer Alex Spiro, threatened to sue Gibney’s production company Jigsaw Productions for defamation. This claim centers on how the documentary supposedly promotes a debunked conspiracy theory that Musk used his Starlink satellites to help Donald Trump secure votes in the 2024 presidential election. Bleecker Street has appealed to the social media sites over the rejected trailer ads. YouTube and TikTok are still considering the appeal, while Meta, which owns Facebook and Instagram, has already rejected them. Musk has a lengthy track record of silencing his critics. After taking over X, formerly Twitter, he spuriously banned journalists that were critical of him, and he’s often resorted to using hush money to keep a bad word from being spoken about him. Private List Warns Woman of “Over a Hundred” Allegedly Predatory Men in Silicon Valley

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Silicon Valley has apparently become so rife with seedy characters that tech workers are now distributing a spreadsheet of “over a hundred” alleged sexual predators in the industry, the New York Post reports. The list isn’t publicly available, but is said to contain “prominent investors and businessmen,” according to the paper’s sources, and is a way of warning others. “Some of them were very prominent names, you know? People who were famous investors,” one up-and-coming woman in tech told the Post. “Businessmen who were in the scene, I had seen them at parties before.” The list, on top of names, is said to contain details of alleged acts of abuse which include drugging, sexual assaults, and rape. But it’s very selectively distributed, probably in part to protect the people who’ve made the allegations. “I met some women who ran the ‘whisper network,’ it has hundreds of cases,” one woman who was asked to add her own account to the list after sharing it with others, told the Post. “I only found out about it because I started asking around.” Women in the industry, sources said, often don’t go to the police because they fear retaliation from those they accused, which could damage their careers and professional relationships. “I was worried that if I tried for justice in my case and I lost, I would be blacklisted from every investor in town,” one woman said. News of the list’s circulation comes after renewed attention into the frat-like culture dominating the AI industry. The New York Times recently published an investigation numerous sexual harassment cases and rape allegations stemming from “hacker houses,” large mansions where as many as dozens of ambitious young tech workers live — and oftentimes party — together while pursuing their careers. As much as they are reputed for fostering hedonistic lifestyles, these hacker houses also are typically backed by some of the industry’s top figures, giving them a chance to mingle and network with them. One prominent hacker house called the AGI House racked up 37 incidents logged by police since 2022, and a member of its progenitor house, called Genesis, was accused of rape and assault. Yehong Zhu, who leveled the accusation, told the Post that she was discouraged by a house organizer from going to the police, and that many in the industry refused to help her track down the alleged attacker when he fled. “We invested together. We did business together,” Zhu, who founded the app Zette, said of the people who refused to help or turned their backs on her. AI Bros Are Having a Meltdown Because the Associated Press Stylebook Clarifies That AIs Don’t Have Feelings
2026-09-27

AI Bros Are Having a Meltdown Because the Associated Press Stylebook Clarifies That AIs Don’t Have Feelings

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
In the midst of heated discussions on AI safety and consciousness, the Associated Press Stylebook has reminded everyone of where it stands on the whole “anthropomorphism” debate. “Artificial intelligence systems do not think, feel, want or understand. Avoid language that gives them human characteristics,” it wrote Wednesday in a post on X. “This is called anthropomorphizing, when we ascribe human traits, emotions or behaviors to non-human things, such as animals or inanimate objects.” This didn’t go over well in the world of AI experts and adherents, whose nerves, shall we say, were collectively struck by what they characterized as a “completely insane” declaration. “I’m not sure a *style book* should be making such confident metaphysical pronouncements,” fumed Alan Rozenshtein, a member of technical staff at Anthropic. Henry Shevlin, a philosopher and AI ethicist at Google DeepMind, suggested that the AP was being hypocritical because it anthropomorphized none other than Optimus Prime — the fictional, sapient robot from the “Transformers” franchise — in a film review. “An AP movie review can tell us Optimus Prime mourns and loves, but readers need protecting from ‘the computer understands,'” he wrote. “We routinely use mentalistic language without literal commitment. You’d hope a style guide could distinguish using words from believing things.” OpenAI’s head of strategic futures Dean Ball was also indignant. “AIs *clearly* think and understand,” he averred. “If your definition of ‘think’ excludes entities that can autonomously resolve Millennium Problems, it is your definition that is flawed,” he added, referring to how OpenAI’s models found a solution to a famous math problem (a claim that itself is fraught with controversy). “This desire to avoid anthropomorphic language has become a pathology,” he added. It’s true that there’s some debate among philosophers and scientists over whether AI models think or feel, but even saying that’s probably lending the AI-is-sentient-camp a little too much credence. Fascinating a possibility as it is to consider, there’s no consensus that AI systems have these experiences. It’s a testament to how defensive, if not cultish, that AI advocates have become that merely suggesting that large language models don’t think, let alone in the way humans do, is cause for indignation. Even supposing that it’s possible that AIs are already experiencing human-like emotions, why not err on the side of caution and stick to language that doesn’t bias us in favor of viewing these things as already alive, especially when there’s a trillion dollar industry at work trying to hype its tech as a powerful force that we should let change our lives? New Smart Glasses Scald Users’ Heads With Horrific Temperatures

Futurism | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Smart glasses have been shrouded in controversy for quite some time now. They’ve earned the moniker of “pervert glasses” for allowing creeps to spy on unsuspecting strangers, while sketchy facial recognition software generates fear of a dystopian surveillance state. Meta’s even been hit with a lawsuit for allowing contracts to see users’ intimate moments. The smart glasses world just can’t catch a break. Now, gadget maker Inmo had to recall its Air3 smart glasses because the left arm can “overheat during extended use, posing a risk of serious injury or death due to burn hazard,” according to an official notice by the US Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC). “Consumers should immediately stop using the recalled product and update the product firmware to version 3.16 before reusing the product,” a separate recall notice by Health Canada warned. It’s unclear what exactly is causing the glasses’ right arm to heat up, though it happens to be roughly where the gadget’s integrated 660 mAh battery is located. According to the CPSC, over 1,600 of the pair — which retail from $900 to $1,300 depending on specs — have been sold in the US. Unlike Meta’s AI smart glasses, the Air3 are designed to act as Android-powered augmented reality glasses to be worn while kicking back on the couch, allowing wearers to watch content via the built-in micro OLED display. As The Verge points out, reviews of the Air3 glasses from February noted a bizarre burning sensation. “These glasses retail for nearly $1,100,” YouTube reviewer Non-Tech Tech Guy noted in a video at the time. “For that price, I expected magic. Instead, within the first few minutes, they were literally too hot to keep on my face.” “I’m not talking slightly warm,” he added. “I’m talking hot. It gets to the point where it can actually hurt your ear. They even provide a sleeve in the box to cover the arm. That’s pretty crazy.” “This is clearly a known issue, and their solution is to cover it up,” the reviewer argued. “Not ideal.” More on smart glasses: Australia, the Land of Bluey, Tim-Tams, and Kylie Minogue: Not Having Those Pervert Glasses, Seeks to Pass “World Leading” Ban on Them
