Modulate raises $25M for its voice models and analysis suite
2026-09-28

Modulate raises $25M for its voice models and analysis suite

TechCrunch | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Boston-based voice intelligence startup Modulate has raised $25 in new funding for its platform that uses an array of small models to offer enterprises transcription, emotional analysis, deepfake and AI music detection, and policy enforcement for voice agents in regulated industries. The funding follows a popular trend among investors in the growing voice AI industry: backing companies that are trying to make AI voices sound more human. It also rivals other companies trying to detect the intent behind human conversation by analyzing it, and those trying to protect people and companies from deepfake calls, as it is now easy to clone voices. Modulate’s new funding was led by Future Ventures with participation from Hyperplane and Lakestar. Data from PitchBook indicated that the startup had raised $41 million in funding at a $170 million valuation prior to this round. The startup was founded in 2017 by Mike Pappas and Carter Huffman, who met as MIT physics undergrads. In its early days, the company focused on providing voice modulation for gaming. But later, it started to concentrate on a voice-based moderation tool. With the onset of voice AI models, the company is now concentrating on detecting different sorts of AI audio generation and analyzing intent behind a person’s words. “Our insight into the voice AI space is that a lot of folks are doing transcription, but there’s not really any capability out there that gets the full nuance and full understanding of a conversation, which is so important when you’re talking to another human being,” Huffman said on a call with TechCrunch. The company today runs more than 100 models that are largely categorized into two sections: Signal extraction models to understand vocal emotion, tone, language, and synthetic voice determination; and Analysis/detection models that look at intent, like what the customer is trying to say, whether the caller is violating rules, or whether they are trying to scam the receiver. Huffman said that because it runs smaller models, the company doesn’t need specialized hardware and a ton of compute, which could be crucial when token bills go up. Plus, it’s easier for the company to train models with newer capacities, add them to the lot, and have an orchestrator call them when needed. Modulate has a varied customer base, but it specializes in deepfake detection and alerting organizations like call centers to a possible scam. It also monitors how AI agents respond to customers to assess the quality of calls, along with making sure that AI follows compliance rules in regulatory areas. Because of these products, Module often sits beside the voice stack being used by a company just to analyze calls. As more enterprises adopt AI-powered customer service, it is becoming important for them to know why a customer call was a success or a failure. In that case, gauging customers’ intent and response becomes critical beyond basic analysis. Huffman said Modulate can give granular data to enterprises around that. “I think when companies think of emotion analysis, they think if the customer was neutral or positive, the call was a success, and if the customer was negative, the call was a failure. But actually, many times people will be polite even to, like, AI agents or bots. Right. And they won’t come across as angry, but they’ll be very dissatisfied,” he said. The company said that its tech is also being used to monitor cyberattacks through voice calls. The startup currently has 40-45 employees and aims to add 10 more people in the coming months to bolster model building. Modulate is currently working on increasing its on-premises and on-device deployment capabilities for increased privacy. Insuretech Outmarket raises $34.5M just months after prior round
2026-09-28

Insuretech Outmarket raises $34.5M just months after prior round

TechCrunch | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. Vishal Sankhala led product at a digital life insurance distributor Ethos before it went public earlier this year. In late 2023, the seasoned engineering executive, who had previously worked at Facebook and Uber, left Ethos and launched Outmarket, a startup that uses AI to help insurance agencies and brokers automate all their tedious paperwork. “Ninety-five percent of insurance in the U.S. and worldwide is still sold through [human] agents, and when you look at sort of like how the process is today, it’s very manual,” Sankhala told TechCrunch. “This is a huge opportunity given how massive this industry is.” Outmarket focuses on commercial insurance because these policies involve deep nuances and custom tailoring for every business. Companies must navigate a complex mix of coverage options, ranging from general liability and workers’ compensation to directors and officers (D&O) liability. “There are over 250 different types of coverages that are out there, depending on what business you are and what risk you have and what you want to cover,” Sankhala said. “For each of those, the process is very different. The application forms you need to fill out, the documents you need to read.” Outmarket’s AI automates those time-consuming administrative tasks, helping brokers (namely, organizations like Marsh and Aon that sell insurance) quickly evaluate and recommend the best policies, so they can focus on work that requires a human touch, such as responding to customers during emergencies. That value has attracted over 300 insurance agencies, including 25% among the top 100, as Outcast’s customers since launching a new product 14 months ago.  “For insurance, which is typically not a fast-moving industry, it’s quite fast growth,” Sankhala said. Investors have been impressed with the company, too. Outcast is set to announce that it raised a $34.5 million Series B led by SignalFire, with participation from Fika Ventures, Permanent Capital Ventures, TTV Capital and Dash Fund. The new round, which comes four months after the startup’s $17 million Series A, valued the company at $355 million, according to a person familiar with the investment. The startup is not alone in building an AI operating layer for insurance brokerages. Other startups trying to help insurance brokers include Fulcrum AI and Further AI. But with the U.S. property and casualty insurance market alone over a trillion dollars in annual premiums, Sankhala believes that Outmarket has plenty of room to grow by helping agencies work faster and smarter. ElevenLabs’ new v4 speech model supports more expression control and 90 languages

TechCrunch | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Last day to demo your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt ticket savings of up to $200 + 50% off a second ends Sept 25, 11:59 p.m. PT. REGISTER HERE. ElevenLabs launched two new speech models on Monday, called ElevenLabs v4 and v4 Turbo, offering more expression control, lower latency for voice agents, and support for more than 90 languages. The company released its v3 model last year, and teased the model at an event in Warsaw earlier this year. For the v4 generation of models, ElevenLabs is adopting a new architecture that allows for better control and faster cloning. The company said that with v4, users will be able to clone a voice with just 10 seconds of audio. On the creative side, the model handles voice identity better over longer chunks of text. It also keeps the context of the text in mind while reading it aloud to change expressions. ElevenLabs introduced inline tags to define expression with v3, and is expanding those tags in v4, letting users stack multiple tags and having the model follow the sequence. The previous version supported 70 languages, and ElevenLabs has worked to get that number up to 90 languages with the new version. The startup said that it observed the biggest quality jump in Japanese, Brazilian Portuguese, Mandarin and Cantonese. ElevenLabs has scaled its enterprise calling business rapidly over the last year, with more than 55% of its business coming from large companies. The company said that the new model is suited for voice agents, as the new version has lower latency to allow for more fluid conversation. Plus, the v4 can start generating audio as soon as the LLM behind it starts generating answers. What’s more, the model can also handle confrontations, escalations, and holds differently for better issue resolution. Competition in speech models has ramped up as startups like Cartesia, Deepgram, Fish Audio, Boson, and WellSaid Labs have created expressive speech models. Big companies like Google and OpenAI have also improved their voice models. ElevenLabs raised $500 million earlier this year from Sequoia earlier this year, in a round led by Sequoia that valued the company at $11 billion. There are already rumors of a followup fundraising round that would value the company at $22 billion. ElevenLabs annualized revenue run rate has climbed from roughly $330 million at the start of the year to over $600 million. The company has aggressively hired personnel in different markets such as India, Europe, and Brazil, as its headcount has reached over 800. In a recent interview with TechCrunch, the company’s co-founder and CEO Mati Staniszewski said that the company is aiming for an IPO “in the next years,” but didn’t commit to a timeline. It’s only been a month since AI assistant startup Instinct announced a fundraise that valued it at $2.5 billion, and now the company has already raised another $1 billion, from investors including Sequoia Capital, Benchmark Capital and Coatue, valuing the company at $10 billion. The news of the company’s fundraising efforts was reported earlier this month by The Information. In a press release on Monday, Instinct confirmed this was a Series C round — a pretty quick growth round for a startup that launched its invite-only service in August 2026. The quick fundraises demonstrate the fervor around a new class of consumer AI agents, which can not only answer questions and engage in conversations, but can actually get things done for their users, whether that’s booking travel plans or restaurant reservations, making purchases, paying bills, canceling subscriptions, conducting tedious research, ordering groceries, and more. When asked to perform a task, Instinct uses its own phone number and computer. The company recently rolled out other new features, like “concierge: that can make phone calls for you, to manage things like making appointments at places that don’t offer online booking, as well as a “trusted person network” which allows one person’s Instinct agent to coordinate plans with their friends’ agents. However, these capabilities come at a cost: Some users are questioning the amount of personal information they have to disclose to AI agents to gain access to such capabilities. Instinct’s initial version of its privacy policy was particularly worrisome due to its overreach. The policy has since been updated. Despite its AI assistant’s viral adoption, Instinct is now facing fresh competition from Meta’s own AI assistant, Muse, which offers many similar features, plus a system that can deeply integrate with Meta’s social products. That means Muse can do much of what Instinct does, as well as tasks like monitoring and summarizing your Instagram DMs or Facebook Groups, keeping an eye on Marketplace listings, and more. Such capabilities have sent Muse to the top of the U.S. app stores, where it has been downloaded millions of times. Instinct, which uses SMS and texting to communicate with its users, doesn’t have a mobile app yet. The startup has also not shared its user numbers or any growth metrics, but clearly its investors are seeing something they like. Instinct declined to offer interviews with founder Noah Shinn alongside the fundraising news, but shared a statement attributed to him: “We’re building Instinct to be the best personal agent that can handle the deeply personal nuances of everyday life. U.S. Embassy Issues Security Alert to Citizens in U.K. Amid Suspected Terror Plot

TIME | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Armed police maintain a road block and security checks surrounding RAF Fairford in Gloucestershire, U.K., on Sept. 28, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The U.S. embassy in London has issued a security alert for American citizens in the U.K. following a suspected terror plot near an RAF airbase used by the U.S. military, which led to five arrests on Sunday. “U.S. citizens are encouraged to exercise caution and be mindful of personal security,” read the notice responding to the incident near RAF Fairford. “U.S.-associated facilities, gatherings, and organizations may be at heightened risk. U.S. citizens are advised to follow instructions of local authorities for their personal safety and security.” The alert reminded Americans that the U.K. in April raised its national terrorism threat level to “severe” from “substantial,” a decision made after an antisemitic attack in London was declared a terrorist incident by police. The “severe” level, according to the Metropolitan Police, means “an attack is highly likely.” The warning came hours after British police arrested five men on suspicion of explosives and terrorism offenses after they were detained near RAF Fairford in southwest England, an airbase used by the U.S. to strike Iran, in the early hours of Sunday. Officers made the arrests after receiving a tip-off that three "suspicious vehicles" appeared to be heading toward the direction of the base, which Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) previously said could be a target on account of its use by the U.S. military. The U.K.’s Counter Terrorism Policing (CTP) confirmed in a statement to TIME Monday that the five arrestees were British nationals in their 20s who live in London. “Specially trained CTP investigators have been examining the scene overnight and have been carrying out a range of activity, which have led to multiple new lines of enquiry,” read the statement. British Defense Secretary Wes Streeting told BBC Radio 4’s Today programme early Monday that it is too soon to speculate over the motives during a live investigation. Questioned about a potential Iran link, he said: "We know that Iran and their proxies wish to do us harm. We know that they hate our democracy, and we know that we regularly have to thwart Iranian attempts to threaten either our interests or indeed our allies." Similarly, CTP’s deputy assistant commissioner Vicki Evans said she was “very aware of the levels of speculation about the motivation behind this incident, and the geopolitical questions that are being raised,” but stressed more time is needed to work through the case. The Iranian embassy in London has said it "categorically rejects and strongly condemns" any attempt to "link Iran to" the arrests made near RAF Fairford. "The Circulation of such fabricated speculations... will serve no purpose other than to fuel Iranophobia propaganda in the U.K.," it said Monday. Gloucestershire Police, the local authority responsible for the area that includes the airbase, received a call from a concerned resident at 12:45 a.m. local time on Sunday, regarding three suspicious vehicles traveling toward the airbase. The local resident, a farmer, told police they had found the road blocked by three white vans. “I saw a large group of hooded and masked men. When they saw me, they ran ahead of me… I then realised that something highly unusual was taking place,” she later told the BBC. Armed officers responded to the call and five men were arrested on suspicion of committing offences under the Explosives Act, as well as section 5 of the Terrorism Act. Gloucestershire Police evacuated residents from around 85 nearby households and put a cordon in place while the British Army’s specialist explosive ordnance disposal team examined the vehicles. The incident is now being handled by CTP. While the farmer who alerted police has been hailed for their actions, Streeting has indicated they only had a “partial picture” of the matter and appeared to suggest U.K. security forces had been aware of a suspected plot involving RAF Fairford. U.S. President Donald Trump on Sunday claimed the suspects had been “under view for a long time.” "Working with Britain, it was an amazing job. We had them under investigation. They were looking to do big damage to our fort, and working with the British worked out great… and we got them,” Trump told reporters. When asked if Trump had been correct, Streeting did not reject the assertion. “Our security services and our police, they don’t ask for praise, but I think it was a proud moment yesterday to see the President of the United States recognize the extraordinary work that our police and our security and intelligence services do every day throughout the year and throughout the world to keep us safe,” he said. “That’s all I want to say this morning on that.” TIME has asked the Ministry of Defense and Downing Street whether the British authorities were already investigating a potential plot. Speaking at the Labour Party conference later Monday morning, Streeting celebrated the U.K. and U.S. alliance as “the closest of any two nations on Earth” and vowed the two countries “will always stand together against those who hate our freedom and those who wish us harm.”

Trump's Taxpayer-Funded Ads Draw Bipartisan Criticism
2026-09-28

Trump's Taxpayer-Funded Ads Draw Bipartisan Criticism

TIME | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A screenshot of President Donald Trump's 2023 ad for the 2024 presidential elections. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump declares that “this is the final battle” in one of several taxpayer-funded television ads airing ahead of November’s midterm elections, prompting bipartisan scrutiny over whether the spots violate federal law. The 30-second ad is a black-and-white video of Trump walking down a hall over audio of his warnings against “the deep state,” “warmongers,” “globalists,” “Marxists,” “communists,” and “fascists.” Trump posted the same video on his Truth Social platform in 2023, but with one difference: Then, the monochromatic ad ended with words on screen calling on the viewer to join Trump’s campaign and fine print saying it was “Paid for by Donald J. Trump for President 2024, Inc.” This time, the 2026 ad says at the bottom: “Paid for by the U.S. Government.” CNN reported that the ad aired during at least two NFL games on Sunday. CNBC also reported that the ad aired during Fox News Sunday, citing data from advertising intelligence company AdImpact, adding that the TV ad has cost more than $362,000 so far. A second advert with the same “paid for by the U.S. government” disclaimer began airing on Wednesday. The ad, which featured the song “Love Me” by singer-songwriter JMSN, showed a montage of photos and videos of Trump, intercut with campaign talking points like “largest tax cuts in history” and “reigniting American manufacturing.” This ad, which CNBC reported also aired over the weekend, citing AdImpact data, has cost the federal government nearly $920,000 to date. It also apparently did not get the approval of JMSN, who posted on social media that he “would never authorize my music to be used for ANY political agenda or campaign.” A third, which is a minute long, features scenic views of Mount Rushmore interspersed with parts of Trump’s speech there for the Fourth of July anniversary. The ads appear to be part of a campaign blitz ahead of the November midterms, in which the GOP is hoping to defend its majorities in Congress. Some lawmakers, including from his own Republican Party, are raising concerns with Trump and the White House over the advertisements. Asked about the ads by CBS News on Sunday, Senator John Kennedy of Louisiana said: “I don’t think any public official, including President Trump or Kristi Noem or John Kennedy, should spend public money on private ads for themselves.” (Kennedy was referring to former Homeland Security Secretary Noem, who came under fire for spending $220 million on an advertising campaign for border security.) When asked about the “Love Me” ad, Senator Thom Tillis of North Carolina said that the use of taxpayer money was reminiscent of the far-right former Hungarian Prime Minister Viktor Orbán. “It’s inappropriate,” Tillis told reporters. Rep. Thomas Massie, a Kentucky Republican and a vocal Trump critic, posted on social media about the “Final Battle” ad: “Don’t worry, using taxpayer dollars to run ominous campaign ads of the President has been done before and is completely legal… in banana republics.” Democrats also raised questions. In a Sept. 24 letter to White House Chief of Staff Susie Wiles, Sen. Maggie Hassan of New Hampshire demanded answers on the “Love Me” ad’s production cost, the agencies and external contractors involved, the source of funds, and the legal and ethical bases for the spot. Hassan wrote in her letter that the ad “does not have a clear official government purpose and appears to run afoul of federal prohibitions.” Sen. Andy Kim of New Jersey posted to social media about the black-and-white clip: “How much taxpayer money did the government spend on this ad?” In another Sept. 24 letter, congressional Democrats on appropriations panels wrote to the White House that the “Love Me” ad should be taken off the air, as it was “the sort of government propaganda one might expect in North Korea, not the United States of America, and it is an egregious and illegal misuse of Americans’ hard-earned tax dollars.” But the White House has defended the ads—which it called, in a Sept. 25 press release, “public service announcements.” A White House official told TIME Friday that the ads weren’t political, didn’t have a call to action, and that the President wasn't on the ballot. A spokesperson instead said that the ads were “about reminding Americans to love their country and understand what makes it worth defending, at home, at our borders, and abroad.” Since the 1950s, yearly funding bills—including the appropriations measure the President recently signed—routinely ban the use of taxpayer funds for “publicity or propaganda.” The Government Accountability Office, which investigates how appropriated funds are spent, has interpreted this to refer to material that is “purely partisan,” “self-aggrandizing,” or “covert.” But it also noted that administrations may need to advocate or defend their policies. Some lawmakers have pointed to potential violations of the Hatch Act, the 1939 act that limits the political activities of federal civilian employees in the executive branch. The President is exempt from the Hatch Act, though some argue that White House employees who facilitated the airing of the ads aren’t. Last week, the non-profit advocacy group Public Citizen filed a complaint before the GAO and the Office of Special Counsel, which is responsible for overseeing Hatch Act violations, seeking investigation and enforcement of relevant laws. In defense of the “public service announcements,” the White House has pointed to previous ad campaigns during the administrations of George W. Bush, Barack Obama, and Joe Biden. (During the Bush Administration, one campaign did violate the anti-propaganda measure.) “When prior Administrations utilized the airwaves, it was called ‘public education,’” the Trump White House said. “When this Administration promotes pride in our country, many of those same voices call it ‘propaganda.’”

Money Reveals What Kind of Friend You Are
2026-09-28

Money Reveals What Kind of Friend You Are

TIME | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings There is a scene in season two of Friends I think about often. Rachel, Phoebe, and Joey, who have low-paying jobs, decide to have a serious conversation with their more wealthy friends —Ross, Monica, and Chandler—about struggling to pay for expensive group dinners and concert tickets. Ross says he never thinks about money, prompting Rachel’s famous line, “That’s because you have it!” Even though this scene aired in 1995, the same dynamic continues to play out in friendships in 2026. Last year, my friend wanted to have a birthday dinner at Carbone in London, an Italian restaurant that is arguably more famous for celebrity sightings than its actual food. My friend works full-time at a national magazine, whereas I’m a freelancer. Everyone else cancelled on her birthday, so it was just the two of us. I felt bad for her, so I didn’t mind spending $75 on food. But a few months later, when it was time to plan my birthday, I couldn’t imagine asking any of my friends to spend the same amount on me just to visit a fancy restaurant. It felt like an extortionate amount of money. It felt tone deaf and entitled. So I hosted a house party instead and asked everyone to bring pizza. Another friend recently ranted to me about how she has spent more than $10,000 over the last year attending destination weddings and bachelorette parties. And while she is, of course, ultimately responsible for her decision to oblige, she has also noticed that the financial pressure to participate in expensive wedding culture tends to breed unnecessary, quiet resentment among her friends. This pattern is painfully, but subtly, common. Money can shape friendships and also reveal people’s values. The wealth gap between friends doesn’t necessarily cause dramatic fights or resentment. More often, it shows up in the small decisions: where you eat, which holidays you can afford, whether you can join a last-minute night out, or whether suggesting an expensive activity feels embarrassing in the first place. And unlike romantic relationships, where finances are often explicitly discussed, friends rarely sit down and talk about the fact that one person earns significantly more than another. Instead, the person with less money can become the one who compromises or simply starts opting out. The way we handle money with our friends reveals a lot about our values, particularly when there is a significant wealth gap. Someone who can comfortably spend lavishly on a dinner, holiday, or birthday celebration may not realize that they're effectively asking a friend with less disposable income to make a significant financial sacrifice to participate. The problem is widespread. Too often people go into debt in order to attend a friend’s bachelorette. Then there’s Venmo ghosting where people avoid repaying their friends after a shared bill. When friends repeatedly avoid paying someone back, the problem is that one person is comfortable making another person carry the financial burden. Money and conversations around money are taboo in our society, says psychotherapist Silvia Dutchevici, a licensed clinical social worker. “In my practice, I’ve seen patients share their most intense fears and sexual desires with little reservation, and then become visibly uncomfortable when asked about their salary, or naming the amount of a bonus or raise.” Think about it: how many of us actually know how much money our friends have or the salaries they make? Money, it turns out, is never just money. It’s a modern measurement of traits like  freedom, prestige, power, and control. Our society implicitly and explicitly sends the message that money is equivalent to worth. “Neoliberal ideology tells us that financial success is the result of hard work, and that poverty is a personal failure rather than a systemic problem,” points out Dutchevici. When two friends have very different financial circumstances, acknowledging the gap can feel like saying something about who is more deserving, or who is less hard working. Both sides can carry shame. The friend with less money may feel judged or inadequate. Meanwhile, many affluent people also carry a deep shame around wealth, feel undeserving of it, and hide their financial means. “I once worked with a woman set to inherit millions who felt she couldn’t accept it until she had proved her worth by earning her own,” says Dutchevici. It is interesting to witness how wealth inequality is reshaping friendships, particularly as the cost of living continues to rise and people in the same social circles can have wildly different disposable incomes. Many of my friends have had their parents buy them houses, so their entire salary is spent on holidays, shopping, and frivolous hobbies. My paycheck, in contrast, goes towards rent and covering my basic needs. This inherently changes how we spend our days. There is an emotional side of the friendship wealth divide, too: the guilt of saying no, the shame of admitting you can’t afford something, the resentment that can build when one friend gets to choose the restaurant or holiday, and the awkwardness of accepting that in the face of a high price tag. All of these pressures have led me to believe that friendship requires more financial negotiation than it once did. We need new etiquette around money in friendship. What does it look like to make plans that don’t automatically exclude the least wealthy person in the group? Can friends with very different incomes maintain genuine equality? What happens when one person’s idea of a casual dinner is another person’s weekly food budget? These conversations don’t have to be complicated. Last week, my friend who works in radiology picked a sushi restaurant with a $40 per-person menu. “Is $40 okay for you? We can go somewhere else if not,” she said in a casual tone on the phone. I appreciated her thoughtfulness and conscientiousness. The end result: a closer friendship and no resentment. Friendship has its own financial morality. Being a good friend means being conscious of what your choices cost other people, not assuming everyone can afford your lifestyle, paying people back without being chased, and being willing to compromise. The wealth gap simply makes those values much more visible. And money reveals how we treat the people we claim to care about. What to Know About Three Mass Shootings in South Africa
2026-09-28

What to Know About Three Mass Shootings in South Africa

TIME | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Members of the South African National Defence Force (SANDF) stand guard as a South African Police Service (SAPS) officer stretches police tape around the scene where gunmen armed with assault rifles stormed a bar in Johannesburg on Sept. 27, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings South African police have launched manhunts after three mass shootings killed at least 32 people. Police were searching for suspects early Monday after separate shootings took place over the weekend in impoverished townships, including Wedela in West Rand District outside Johannesburg, Lwandle in Western Cape, and Khayelitsha in Western Cape. “Violence and criminality continues to spread in our country,” President Cyril Ramaphosa said on Sunday. “This is the level of criminality that we must bring down, and our security forces are now going to continue pulling out all stops. It cannot go on like this.” He pledged stronger action by security forces to reduce violent crime in the country. The shootings came amid a recent spate of mass shootings and other killings that have alarmed South Africans. Authorities have linked some of the attacks to criminal gangs fighting over territory or illicit businesses like illegal mining, drug trafficking, and extortion. South African police define a mass shooting as a single attack that results in three or more people shot. In March, Ramaphosa deployed soldiers to support police as part of a yearlong operation against gang violence and illegal mining, including operations around Johannesburg. The President described organized crime as the most immediate threat to the country’s democracy and economic development. Some observers, however, noted that the deployment was an overdue attempt to address the police’s failure to keep communities, especially in poorer townships, safe. Read More: What South Africa Can Teach Us as Worldwide Inequality Grows Eighteen people were killed and more than a dozen wounded in a shooting in the township of Wedela on Saturday night. Police said eight men opened fire on patrons drinking outside a tavern at around 9:30 p.m. local time, before entering the bar and “shooting at patrons inside.” The perpetrators allegedly searched some patrons and stole cellphones before escaping in two unidentified vehicles. The attackers allegedly carried pistols and AK-47 rifles. Two vehicles were also burnt during the attack. Seventeen people died at the scene, including 13 men and four women. Police identified two of the deceased victims as South Africans and another two as Lesotho nationals. Fifteen injured victims were transported to hospital for treatment, with several in critical condition. Another victim died in hospital, police said on Monday, bringing the death toll to 18. Gauteng Deputy Police Commissioner Fred Kekana said police had a clear picture of the suspects and their names, according to local media reports. He did not publicly identify the suspects. Kekana said police are investigating the attack as possible retaliation for an August shooting at the same tavern that killed one person and wounded another. One of those killed was wanted in connection with the earlier shooting, he said. Ten people were shot and killed at a nighttime entertainment venue in Lwandle, a township on the outskirts of Cape Town, that began at around 1:46 a.m. local time on Sunday. Police said gunmen entered an entertainment venue selling grilled meat and fired at patrons. Five victims died at the scene and five after being transported to medical facilities. At least 11 others were wounded. Police have launched a manhunt for the suspects, with no arrests announced as of Monday morning. Western Cape Police Commissioner Sizakhele Dyantyi pointed to “business rivalry” as a possible motive for the attack. “People are buying alcohol maybe at cheaper places and they come and drink in this place. But we are still investigating that,” Dyantyi said. Police said they increased deployments in the area after the shooting. Police said the deceased victims would be publicly identified only after relatives were informed. Police are investigating the murder of four people in Khayelitsha, a township in Cape Town, on Saturday. Police responded to reports of a shooting at around 7:15 p.m. local time, and found three men dead with multiple gunshot wounds. Two of the victims were found inside a stationary vehicle and the third beside it. A fourth victim who sustained gunshot wounds died upon arrival at a medical facility, police said. No suspects had been identified or arrested, police said on Sunday morning. South Africa is confronting a spate of mass shootings that has affected some of the country’s poorest communities, even as violent crime declined nationally this year. Contact crime, which includes violence or threats against people, between April and June fell 2.3% compared with the same period in 2025, while murders declined 5.9%. But the country still has among the highest homicide rates in the world. In the same three months, 5,427 people were killed, averaging roughly 60 murders a day, according to police data. Organized crime researchers have raised alarm about the rise in mass shootings. Julian Rademeyer, an adviser for the Global Initiative Against Transnational Organized Crime, told the Newzroom Afrika broadcaster that the organization has tracked 41 mass killings resulting in around 196 deaths since January. The Associated Press counted seven mass shootings that had drawn national headlines since December 2025, six of which involved multiple gunmen. The country’s poorest communities bear the brunt of the violence. After a mass shooting in June that killed 12 people in an informal settlement of shacks in Johannesburg, the AP reported that many of the country’s impoverished townships have inadequate lighting, slow police responses, and a lack of security. Those townships at times border affluent suburbs with armed private security controls and substantially lower violent crime rates, highlighting the country’s deep economic divide. There are also more than 2 million illegal guns in the country despite strict gun ownership regulations, according to civil society groups. Allegations of police corruption have also undermined public trust: allegations that top police officials were colluding with organized criminals prompted Ramaphosa to announce a national investigation last year into police corruption, which has led to more than a dozen arrests of senior officers. Illegal mining gangs are also a notorious problem in Johannesburg, and often establish their bases in poorer areas with lower policing. The government said last year that illegal mining had cost South Africa more than $3 billion in 2024. Last month, residents in Wedela reportedly described living in fear of rival illegal mining gangs in the area. On Tuesday night, three gunmen opened fire at a house where around 14 people were gathered for a party in KwaMakhutha, south of the city of Durban. The shooting killed 12 people, including a woman believed to be pregnant. Following the attack, KwaZulu-Natal premier Thami Ntuli raised concerns about illegal guns and drugs in the area, noting that residents hear gunshots every night.

Nursing Homes Have Become Last-Resort Housing
2026-09-27

Nursing Homes Have Become Last-Resort Housing

TIME | 2026-09-27

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings As an occupational therapist working in medical respite care, Caitlin Synovec saw people in their 50s or 60s at a moment when their lives had forever shifted. Perhaps a stroke or other serious medical event sent them to the hospital. But too often, during their extended treatment, they lost their jobs and then their housing. Newly homeless, they might manage to snag a spot in a respite care program designed for people without stable housing who need a place to continue their recovery. But then, a question would loom over their future: Where would they go next? Many patients in that situation wind up living on the streets or in shelters, despite efforts to find them permanent housing. A surprising number of older adults, however, find themselves in a nursing home—even if they don’t need or want that level of care. According to research from the United Health Foundation, one in 11 people in nursing homes have “low-care needs,” meaning they do not require physical assistance for bed mobility, transferring, using the toilet, or eating. The ratios vary by state, from a low of 2.5% in Hawaii to nearly 23% in Oklahoma. It’s an expensive housing solution: One year in a nursing home costs $119,340 on average, compared to around $16,000 per person per year for subsidized permanent supportive housing that includes some services. Synovec recalls one client in her 50s who lost her housing while she was in the hospital getting treatment for a stroke. Although a family member was willing to take her in, she couldn’t navigate their second-floor apartment. The stroke left her without the use of one arm and with mild cognitive problems, but she still was able to manage most daily activities, including taking the bus to visit family, friends, and her church. She needed some help—for example, with getting dressed—but she valued her independence. “She didn’t want to be institutionalized,” says Synovec, who is now assistant director of medical respite at the National Health Care for the Homeless Council. “She wanted to be in the community.” The woman went to a homeless shelter, but then she fell and ended up in the emergency room. The hospital discharged her to a nursing home. Many emergency shelters aren’t equipped for the elderly, even though older adults are the fastest-growing segment of the homeless population. Shelters often house people in bunk beds. Their bathrooms typically aren’t designed for people with disabilities. And shelter residents must leave early each morning and fend for themselves until the shelter reopens in the evening. The better option—permanent supportive housing—has an average wait list of over two years, and even that housing may lack accessibility for elderly people with mobility issues. Under the Trump Administration’s shift away from permanent supportive housing toward time-limited transitional housing, wait lists are expected to grow, and some formerly homeless older adults could be displaced. “They get to a point essentially where there’s not a place in the community for them to go,” says Synovec. “The option is long-term care—nursing home care—or they die on the street.” Gaps in the social safety net are widening as Baby Boomers age, says Dennis P. Culhane, a University of Pennsylvania social science researcher with a focus on housing policy and homelessness. In 2025, one out of every five people living in emergency shelters or on the streets was 55 or older. “We’re failing in every direction in terms of not providing shelter—exposing people to greater harm and health risk and disability,” Culhane says. As a social worker and researcher, Ian Johnson interviewed dozens of social workers, nurses, and other professionals in Seattle who were helping unhoused older adults cope with serious medical conditions. Many of the older adults feared being in a more restrictive environment if they went to a nursing home, and nurses and social workers told of patients who chose to return to the streets from a hospital or rehab unit rather than enter long-term nursing home care. That’s a common sentiment. Three out of four older adults want to age in their current home and community, according to surveys by AARP, an advocacy organization for older Americans. “Early institutionalization” often separates people from their family, friends, and neighborhood. “That is not different for people living in supportive housing or sometimes living in a tent in the woods or at a homeless shelter,” says Johnson, who is now at Boston University School of Social Work. “People want to spend their end of life still having a sense of self and a sense of autonomy.” At the same time, stories also abound of nursing homes discharging vulnerable older adults to homeless shelters—or to no destination at all. The triggering event could be a violation of rules, such as smoking or substance use, or depletion of insurance. Those opposing trends don’t make much sense, except in the context of cost pressures and sparse options. When short-term rehabilitation care ends, patients are expected to pay on their own or leave. Medicaid covers long-term nursing home care, but state eligibility rules vary. Some people who just need parttime help with personal care and medication may end up lingering in a nursing home, covered by Medicaid. In other cases, despite federal rules about safe discharges, nursing homes have dropped off residents at shelters. One answer to this predicament is to build subsidized housing that meets the mobility and care needs of older adults with very low incomes but preserves their sense of independence. In other words, age-friendly, low-income housing would give people who are aging and homeless the “4 Ms” framework that seeks to address mobility, medication, mentation, and what matters most to older adults. Finding the right care for older adults facing homelessness could be part of a broader rethinking of long-term care. Dr. Jeffrey Farber, a geriatrician who is president and CEO of The New Jewish Home in New York City, has written that “nursing homes serve three distinct populations of older adults—and only one of those actually belongs in what we think of as a nursing home.” Hospitals tend to discharge patients as soon as they’re medically stable, so nursing homes, also known as skilled nursing facilities, became providers of short-term rehabilitation. When they leave rehab, most patients rely on family caregivers or paid aides for any ongoing help. “Some of those patients, when the system breaks down and there’s no place for them to go that’s safe and affordable, they just end up staying” in the nursing home, Farber told me. “They’re benefiting from some services that we provide, but they certainly could be cared for in a lower-acuity setting or a lower-cost setting.” Farber envisions a remake: Hospitals would provide the sub-acute care and only discharge patients when they are ready to go home. People who are homeless and aging with manageable medical conditions would have access to subsidized housing and special programs tailored to their needs. The rest would receive long-term monitoring and care for their complex medical needs at a transformed version of nursing homes that he would rebrand as Compassionate Care Homes, “always non-profit, mission-driven.” He added that these homes must be funded adequately, with investment in workforce training. The oldest Baby Boomers turn 80 in 2026. By 2030, every Baby Boomer will be 65 or older. Just as they have impacted so many aspects of society, they will overwhelm the capacity for senior housing, long-term care, and emergency shelter. It never made sense to house people with low-care needs in nursing homes, but this wave will make doing so unsustainable. Farber wants to spark a conversation about transforming long-term care and the way it’s funded. “The system is fundamentally broken,” he says, “and we’re not going to fix it with band-aids.” TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Taction Technology is a San Diego-based audio technology company that was founded and incorporated around 2014. The Patents Involved in the Apple LawsuitThe high-profile patent infringement lawsuit centers on tactile transducer technology (utilizing dampening systems, magnets, and ferrofluid to handle low-frequency audio and haptic feedback). The foundational patents were created, filed, and issued within the following timeframes: U.S. Patent No. 10,659,885 ("Systems and methods for generating damped electromagnetically actuated planar motion for audio-frequency vibrations"): Filed on October 3, 2019, and officially issued on May 19, 2020.U.S. Patent No. 10,820,117: A closely related continuation patent sharing the same core technical specification, issued later in 2020.*(Note: Taction originally filed the federal lawsuit against Apple in 2021, which recently went to trial resulting in a major jury verdict.) Now wait, when did Apple add haptics to the Apple Watch?Apple's First Device with HapticsApple Watch (2014): Apple first introduced its proprietary Taction Engine (branded as the Taptic Engine) in the original Apple Watch released in late 2014 to simulate physical taps on the wrist.iPhone 6s (2015): The technology was brought to the iPhone lineup the following year with the iPhone 6s and 6s Plus. So Taptics didn't start operating as a company until the time Apple built haptics into the Apple Watch. And they didn't file for a patent until 5 years later. That's sus. Sony: Released the DualShock controller for the original PlayStation in 1997, which introduced built-in dual eccentric rotating mass (ERM) motors to deliver rumble feedback. (Note: While Nintendo introduced the plug-in Rumble Pak accessory earlier in 1997, Sony's DualShock was the first mainstream controller to build native haptic motors directly into the standard hardware).Microsoft: Included dual rumble motors right from its entry into the console market with the launch of the original Xbox ("The Duke" controller) in 2001. [fool.com] Bloomberg reported that Oracle has sent a legal notice to the developer of its $X billion "Project Jupiter" AI data center campus scheduled to come online in 2028. It cites "force majeure," a contract provision that offers protection if events outside of a company's control disrupt a deal -- in this case, the delay of a critical natural gas pipeline. Oracle is hoping to delay rent payments if the data center is not delivered by the deadline. The developer, a subsidiary of Blue Owl Capital, must first agree that a qualifying event occurred, and Oracle would still be on the hook for certain fees and other payments. While Oracle insists the project is on schedule, investors are growing increasingly worried. The $18 billion in project financing for Poject Jupitor have fallen into "stressed" territory. That means the banks that loaned the money are willing to let them go for considerably less than they are worth in name, fearing the loans may go bad. The Financial Times reported that as of last Friday, they had fallen to around 89 to 91 cents on the dollar. That's in part due to the stiff local opposition the 1,400-acre campus is facing. Residents are concerned with the environmental impact as well as noise pollution -- a common theme from projects around the country. The accounting trick this time is that the contractual commitments don't show up when they are made. The pack of hyperscalers is holding $1.2 trillion in future lease commitments along with $1.5 trillion in commitments to purchase AI-related hardware and equipment, such as memory chips. The latter can be kept off a firm's balance sheet until the products ultimately reach the hyperscalers and each party fulfills its obligations under current accounting rules. The statute explicitly spells out "intentionally", "deliberately", etc over and over. The remedy is civil, not criminal. And just to preempt this too: civil does not mean "mild". Civil law absolutely can kill companies, even large ones, if they damage they've done is big enough. And even when the cost is not sufficient on its own, courts allow juries to consider net worth of the defendant so that the penalty has sufficient sting to discourage the defendant and others from repeating said conduct. And remember: that vertical axis is a logarithmic scale. As for "the price of a lollypop per square meter", that's literally the first time I've ever heard that phrase, but okay, I'll bite. If by "lollipop" you mean retail prices for one of those big lollies [amazon.com], they're like $11 on Amazon on average. PV wafers are about $0,045/W [trendforce.com]. At a typical ~250W/m, that's like.... $11. Yes, a square meter of PV wafers is about the cost of a lollipop! Yes, integrating them into whatever panels or other solar device increases cost over the raw wafers, but we very much are "on the order of lollies". The perovskite semiconductors here are a very different technology than the old thin photovoltaics. They have the potential to be much more efficient, and the deposition techniques are cheap and comparatively low-tech Whether they can succeed in simultaneously be highly-efficient, resistant to degradation in the environment, and still be low cost is the subject of a lot of work. We'll see. They were supposed to be manufactured for the price of a lollypop per square meter back in the '90s.To be more specific, the target back in the '80s and '90s was clearly articulated as being fifty cents per watt (where "per watt" meant, when illuminated at standard conditions of 1 kW/square meter.) Silicon photovoltaics can now be purchased at 12 cents per watt. [techspot.com] In today's dollars. They not only hit the target, they blew it away.The reason the thin films of the '80s and '90s lost was not that they were bad, but that silicon simply outcompeted them.So forgive my skepticism regarding new breakthroughs.I have some amount of skepticism too. The reason the thin films of the '80s and '90s lost was not that they were bad, but that silicon simply outcompeted them. A Delightful New Way to Flip a Deep-Red District
2026-09-28

A Delightful New Way to Flip a Deep-Red District

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-28

Visit Original Website
This November, Bobby Pulido is trying to flip a Republican congressional seat blue, right on the Texas border. That would be reason enough to talk to him. But he was famous way before politics—Pulido is a Tejano star, a 1990s teen idol, a Tejano Backstreet Boy before there were the Backstreet Boys. He’s also a lifelong resident of the Rio Grande Valley: “My dad was a migrant farmworker, and so was his dad. We don’t come from generational wealth,” he said. “Latinos and Hispanics in South Texas are the embodiment of the American dream.” Over the years, he has watched friends and neighbors pivot from the Democratic Party to the GOP. Donald Trump’s inroads with Latino voters were one of the surprises of his 2024 victory, as he carried places like South Texas’ 15th District. But Pulido thinks those gains are temporary, and that Democrats have a chance, with sensible policies, to retake the district and Congress. For him, immigration enforcement is a top priority. “President Obama did crack down on it, that’s a fact,” he said. “But I think under Biden, it got really out of hand.” With Trump, he says, “We went from ‘Let them all in’ to ‘Kick them all out.’ And neither one of the two things work.” On a recent episode of What Next, host Mary Harris spoke to Pulido about what Democrats misunderstand about South Texas and whether he’s singing the right tune for Congress. This conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Mary Harris: I was wondering why you would run for office. You’re a well-known Tejano singer. You probably don’t need to do much of anything if you don’t want to. Bobby Pulido: I was a political science major in college. I was a Texas Boys Stater in high school. Government and social studies and Texas history were my favorite subjects. I wanted to do this. You’re running in Texas’ 15th District, a district that Donald Trump won by nearly 18 points in 2024. Redistricting may have made it a smidge Trumpier, but it relies on the Latino vote remaining in the GOP’s favor. And my assumption is that part of the reason you got into this race is that you were beginning to see signs that that was not going to hold. I’m curious what those first signs were to you. I was talking to lots of people during this cycle—even the cycle before, as it was going right since ’23. Lots of my friends were voting red. And, listen, I’ve always been a blue-dog Democrat. I was going to ask about that, whether you always considered yourself a Democrat. You have all these things that don’t read as Democrat. You’re known for long-range shooting. You named your eldest son Remington, after the rifle. You’re not a traditionally coded Democrat. That’s based on a newer version of what a Democrat is. See, I never changed. I’ve been the same type. All the people that used to vote Democrat are like me, and they started voting red. When I would talk to them, the one thing that jumped out at me was that they didn’t like the leftward shift on a lot of Democratic policies. Oh, jeez, like guns. Listen, we’re on the border. My district covers 11 counties, and 10 of the counties other than the Rio Grande Valley are rural. Firearms are not viewed the same way in the city as they are in the country. How are they seen differently? As sport and protection? Both. It’s an independence. When you’re out in the country, first of all, there’s no traffic. You’re not dealing with a lot of people to begin with. You can drive faster if you want to. I’m not advocating for speeding, but you’re not bound by the confines of traffic, where you have a stoplight at every block. It’s a different way of life. And yes, there’s less things to do. When you’re out in these rural areas, the thing where you go out the most, to get dressed, is to go to church on Sunday. A lot of the sports that you practice, whether that be hog hunting, coyote hunting, or just hunting, they’re associated with firearms. Down here in the Valley, the No. 1 fundraiser for Democratic events are skeet shoots. I can’t make this up. You shoot, and there’s sporting clays. Gun sports are a way of life. I saw an old interview with Chuck Schumer talking about: “We’ll take the city vote over the rural votes because I’ll take those 2-to-1.” That was a huge mistake. I’m insulted by hearing him say that, because if I look down the road, in 2030, California and New York are going to lose congressional seats to Texas and Florida, because people are moving out to the rural areas because it’s cheaper to live. As a Democrat, if we want to make people’s lives better and not let these batshit crazy people like Trump get in there, we’re going to have to moderate on some issues to understand rural folk a little better. What happened was when the Green New Deal was promoted—the oil and gas business, it’s probably one of the best-paying jobs in my district, and it got really politicized, where it was like, “They’re going to take away your job.” That’s how it was being sold: The Democrats were going to take away your jobs. You’re not going to be able to provide for your family. So when did you see it turn back? You’re saying you saw a lot of your friends flip or maybe express more GOP-coded opinions in terms of how they were voting. When do you see that change back to maybe thinking there was an opportunity here? It wasn’t a switch. I was talking to people, and they would say, “Look, Bobby, I’m voting Republican, but you’re my kind of Democrat. I would vote for you because you’re not batshit crazy.” Because they painted Democrats as very radical, and it worked. How much did immigration play a role? Even back in February, I was looking at Politico, for instance, writing about your district specifically and talking about how Republican Monica De La Cruz, your opponent, was all of a sudden like, “Oh, no, I need to moderate my opinions here,” because people in her district were. Well, here’s the thing: Immigration is really a two-tiered issue down here. One thing is the border, and the other is enforcement in the streets. There’s a difference. The border got really chaotic with asylum-seekers. Now, the diaspora in the Rio Grande Valley and South Texas in general is Mexican, Mexican American. Mexican Americans cannot claim political asylum because you’re in a neighboring country. So, historically, most of the people here, their grandfather or their great-grandfather crossed the border illegally, undocumented, and they ran away from the Border Patrol and had to figure out life on their own. And it was like, hey, I’ve got to cut yards, I’ve got to do whatever I can to provide for my family. Yeah, you talked about your grandfather being like, “I was so proud I never took a penny from the government.” So you’re talking about the same kind of spirit. That’s exactly right. That was the spirit of my grandfather, and that’s the spirit of this region. We’re proud people. Probably our forte is that we work really hard and we’ll figure it out. What you started seeing is when the asylum process started getting overwhelmed, it was catch and release. An asylum-seeker would not run away from a Border Patrol agent—they would run to them and say, “I want to declare political asylum.” It was backlogged, so they would fly them in-country to wherever they wanted to go. I want to make clear that it’s not my view, but the sentiment I was hearing often was: “I see everybody here at the airport flying wherever the hell they want. My grandfather couldn’t do that.” And it really pissed people off. Well, a lot of Latinos at the border work in enforcement too. That’s true. There’s a lot of people working in Border Patrol and law enforcement, and they were really angry with the Biden administration. That had a lot to do with why the shift happened so big. And what’s happening now, with the enforcement in the streets, is I’ve got Republicans who go: “My gardener’s gone, and I can’t find anybody to cut my yard or clean my house, or build houses.” There was a housing bill that Trump refused to sign that went in because, after the 10-day period, it became law. That makes no change down here if you don’t have a workforce. There’s not a building in the Rio Grande Valley that was built without immigrant labor. Not one. I don’t care what anybody wants to tell me. I will argue that to the day I die. I think that a lot of people might make the assumption that a Latino population might look at images of people on the street being captured by ICE, what we’re seeing now, and have a very emotional reaction to it, which I’m sure they are as well, but you’re really framing a lot of the concerns as economic. It’s both. I will tell you, I’ve led with the humanitarian thing. It breaks my heart to see the things that are happening. And here’s the other thing: People don’t really understand us down here, but the Rio Grande Valley is a lot of mixed-status families. Recently, a friend of mine, a three-time Trump voter, 65 years old, eighth-generation Texan from an oil and gas family—I was there years ago when he met his now wife, who was an immigrant. They came over, she overstayed her visa, they met, they fell in love. They have an 8-year-old American daughter. He’s trying to get her papers, and there was a technicality in there—she had crossed over and not said it and is getting deported. My friend is going to have to leave the country, and potentially, like he says, die in a country that’s not his. The thing that people are not really taking into account is it’s affecting everybody here. This is not us vs. them. It’s affecting our community.
